# PREFACE.

I wrote this Treatise during my stay in France twelve years ago; and I communicated it in the year 1678 to the learned persons who then composed the <term>Royal Academy of Sciences</term> <note>The primary scientific institution of France, established by Louis XIV in 1666</note>, to which the King had done me the honor of calling me. Several members of this body, who are still alive, will be able to remember having been present when I gave the reading of it, and better than the others, those among them who applied themselves particularly to the study of <term>Mathematics</term>; of whom I can now only cite the celebrated Gentlemen, <term>Cassini</term> <note>Giovanni Domenico Cassini, known for discovering the division in Saturn's rings</note>, <term>Romer</term> <note>Ole Rømer, the first to measure the speed of light</note>, and <term>De la Hire</term> <note>Philippe de La Hire, a prominent astronomer and mathematician</note>. And although since then I have corrected and changed several places therein; the copies that I had made at that time could serve as proof that I have nevertheless added nothing to it, if not some conjectures concerning the formation of <term>Iceland Crystal</term> <note>A transparent variety of calcite used to study the polarization of light</note>, and a new remark on the <term>refraction</term> <note>The bending of light as it passes from one substance to another</note> of <term>Rock Crystal</term>. I wished to report these particulars to make known how long I have meditated upon these things

<summary>In this preface, Christiaan Huygens establishes that his treatise on light was originally composed in 1678 and presented to the French Royal Academy of Sciences, citing famous colleagues as witnesses to its early development.</summary>
<keywords>Royal Academy of Sciences, mathematics, Cassini, Romer, De la Hire, Iceland Crystal, refraction, Rock Crystal, scientific priority</keywords>

# P R E F A C E .

...things that I am now publishing, and not to detract from the merit of those who, without having seen anything of what I had written, may have happened to treat similar matters: as indeed occurred with two excellent <term>Geometers</term> <note>In this period, "geometer" was often used as a general term for a mathematician.</note>, Messrs. Newton and Leibniz, regarding the problem of the shape of lenses for collecting rays when one of the surfaces is given.

One might ask why I have delayed so long in bringing this work to light. The reason is that I had written it rather carelessly in the language in which it is now seen <note>The author refers to French, his native tongue, rather than the scholarly Latin he originally intended.</note>, with the intention of translating it into Latin, doing so to pay closer attention to the subject matter. After which, I proposed to release it together with another treatise on <term>Dioptrics</term> <note>The study of the refraction of light, particularly through lenses.</note>, where I explain the effects of telescopes and what else pertains to that science. But the pleasure of novelty having ceased, I have deferred from time to time the execution of this design, and I do not know when I would have been able to finish it, being often distracted either by business or by some new study. Considering this, I finally judged that it was better to let this writing appear as it is, than to let it run the risk, by waiting longer, of remaining lost.

One will see here those kinds of demonstrations, which

<summary>In this preface, Christiaan Huygens explains the delay in publishing his work on light, acknowledging the independent discoveries of Newton and Leibniz while justifying his decision to publish in French rather than Latin.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's philosophical justification for his scientific method, specifically addressing the transition from mathematical certainty to physical probability.</meta>

-> # P R E F A C E . <-

do not produce a certainty as great as those of Geometry <note>original: "Geometrie"</note>, and which even differ from them greatly, since whereas Geometers prove their Propositions by certain and incontestable Principles, here the Principles are verified by the conclusions drawn from them; the nature of these things not allowing it to be done otherwise. It is possible, however, to arrive at a degree of probability <note>The author uses "vraisemblance" (likeliness), a key concept in 17th-century science where absolute proof was not always possible.</note> which very often is hardly inferior to full evidence. Namely, when the things demonstrated by these assumed Principles relate perfectly to the phenomena <note>original: "phenomenes"</note> that experience <note>In this context, "experience" refers to experimental observation.</note> has brought to light; especially when there is a great number of them, and even more principally when one conceives of and foresees new phenomena which must follow from the hypotheses <note>original: "hypotheses"</note> employed, and finds that in this the effect corresponds to our expectation. If all these proofs of probability are met in what I have proposed to treat, as it seems to me they are, it must be a very great confirmation of the success of my research, and it is hardly possible that things should not be almost exactly as I represent them. I choose to believe, therefore, that those who love to know Causes, and who know how to admire the marvel of Light <note>original: "Lumiere"</note>, will find some satisfaction in these various speculations which

<summary>The author argues that scientific hypotheses are validated when their predictions match experimental observations, achieving a high degree of probability that rivals mathematical certainty. He introduces his work as an investigation into the causes and marvels of light.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the preface of the work, following the author's explanation of his wave theory of light. The catchword "TA" likely refers to the beginning of the first chapter, "Table of Contents" or "Treatise."</meta>

# P R E F A C E .

regarding it, and in the new explanation of its notable property <note>Huygens refers to the laws of refraction and reflection</note>, which forms the primary foundation for the construction of our eyes, and for those great inventions <note>A reference to telescopes and microscopes</note> which so greatly extend their use. I also hope that there will be those who, by following these beginnings, will penetrate further into this entire subject than I have been able to do, since it is far from being exhausted. This is evident from the places I have marked, where I leave difficulties without resolving them; and even more so by the things I have not touched upon at all, such as the various kinds of Luminous Bodies <note>original: "Corps Luisants"; referring to phosphorescence or bioluminescence</note>, and everything regarding Colors; in which no one until now can boast of having succeeded. Finally, there remains much more to be sought concerning the nature of Light than I claim to have discovered, and I shall owe a great deal in return to whoever can supply what I lack here in knowledge.

->At The Hague. January 8, 1690.<-

<summary>In this concluding section of the preface, Christiaan Huygens acknowledges the limitations of his work, specifically noting that he has not yet solved the mysteries of color or luminous bodies, and invites future scientists to build upon his foundations.</summary>

# TREATISE
# ON LIGHT.
## CHAP. I.
### ON RAYS EXTENDED DIRECTLY.

The demonstrations concerning <term>Optics</term> <note>The branch of physics that studies the behavior and properties of light.</note>, as happens in all sciences where <term>Geometry</term> is applied to matter, are founded upon truths drawn from experience; such as those that the rays of light extend in a straight line; that the angles of reflection and incidence are equal; and that in refractions the ray is broken <note>original: "rompu." In the 17th century, "breaking" a ray was the standard way to describe what we now call refraction, or the bending of light as it passes through different materials.</note> according to the <term>Rule of Sines</term> <note>Now commonly known as Snell’s Law, which calculates how much light bends when entering a medium like glass or water.</note>, by now so well known, and which is no less certain than the preceding ones.

The majority of those who have written concerning the different parts of Optics have been content to presuppose these truths. But some more inquisitive minds have wanted to seek out their origin and causes, considering them in themselves as wonderful effects of Nature. In which, having advanced ingenious things, though not such yet that the most intelligent do not desire explanations that satisfy them more; I wish to propose here what I have meditated upon this subject, to contribute as much as I can to the clarification of this part of <term>Natural Science</term> <note>A precursor to the modern term "physics," referring to the study of the physical world.</note>, which not without reason is reputed to be one of its most difficult. I acknowledge being much indebted to those who first began to dissipate the strange obscurity in which these

<summary>In this opening chapter, Christiaan Huygens introduces his scientific approach to light, grounding his geometric proofs in experimental truths like the straight-line travel of light and the Law of Sines.</summary>
<keywords>optics, geometry, light rays, reflection, refraction, Rule of Sines, natural science</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction from the previous page, where the author began discussing the need for a clearer explanation of the laws of optics based on physical causes rather than just geometric assumptions.</meta>

->T R E A T I S E<-

<meta>catchword: du-</meta>

...things were shrouded, and to give hope that they could be explained by intelligible reasons. But I am also astonished, on the other hand, how those very same authors have very often wished to present poorly-founded reasoning as being very certain and demonstrative; I find that no one has yet successfully explained these first and notable phenomena of light: namely, why it only extends along straight lines, and how visual rays, coming from an infinite number of different places, cross one another without interfering with each other at all.

I shall therefore attempt in this book, through principles accepted in the <term>Philosophy of today</term> <note>This refers to "Mechanical Philosophy," a 17th-century movement that sought to explain all natural phenomena through the motion and collision of physical particles.</note>, to give clearer and more plausible reasons, first for these properties of light extended in a straight line; secondly for that which is reflected by encountering other bodies. Then I shall explain the behavior of rays which are said to undergo <term>refraction</term> <note>The bending of light as it passes from one substance to another, like from air into water.</note> when passing through <term>diaphanous bodies</term> <note>Transparent materials.</note> of different kinds; where I shall also treat the effects of the refraction of the air caused by the different densities of the Atmosphere.

Next, I shall examine the causes of the strange refraction of a certain <term>Crystal brought from Iceland</term> <note>Now known as "Iceland Spar" or calcite, this crystal was famous among 17th-century scientists for its "double refraction," where a single ray of light enters the crystal and splits into two.</note>. And lastly, I shall treat the different shapes of transparent and reflecting bodies, by which rays are gathered at a point or diverted in various ways. Here one will see the ease with which, following our new Theory, one finds not only the <term>Ellipses, Hyperboles</term>, and other curved lines that Mr. <term>Descartes</term> <note>René Descartes (1596–1650), the French philosopher and mathematician who had previously proposed a theory of light and established the law of refraction.</note> subtly invented for this purpose, but also those which must form the surface of a glass lens when the other surface is already given as spherical, flat, or of whatever shape it may be.

One cannot doubt that light consists of the movement of a certain matter. For whether one looks at its pro-

# ON LIGHT. CHAPTER I.

A 2

<meta>catchword: of a</meta>

[intro]duction, one finds that here on Earth it is mainly fire and flame that generate it, which undoubtedly contain bodies that are in rapid motion, since they dissolve and melt many other very solid bodies: whether one looks at its effects, one sees that when light is collected, as by concave mirrors, it has the power to burn like fire, that is to say, it disunites the parts of bodies; which surely indicates movement, at least in <term>true Philosophy</term><note>Huygens refers to "Mechanical Philosophy," the 17th-century scientific view that all natural phenomena can be explained by the motion and collision of physical particles.</note>, in which one conceives of the cause of all natural effects by mechanical reasons. This must be done in my opinion, or else one must renounce all hope of ever understanding anything in <term>Physics</term><note>At this time, "Physics" or "Natural Philosophy" referred to the study of the underlying causes of nature.</note>.

And as, following this Philosophy, it is held for certain that the sensation of sight is excited only by the impression of some movement of a matter that acts on the nerves at the back of our eyes, this is yet another reason to believe that light consists in a movement of the matter that is found between us and the luminous body.

Furthermore, when one considers the extreme speed with which light spreads in all directions, and that when it comes from different places, even entirely opposite ones, they pass through each other without hindering one another; one well understands that when we see a luminous object, it could not be by the transport of a matter, which from that object comes all the way to us, just as a ball or an arrow crosses the air: for certainly that is too contrary to these two qualities of light, and especially to the latter.

It is therefore in another way that it spreads, and what can lead us to understand it is the knowledge we have of the extension of Sound in the air.

We know that by means of the air, which is an invisible and impalpable body, Sound spreads all around the place where it has been produced, by a movement which passes successively from one

<summary>Huygens argues that light must be a form of motion rather than a physical projectile, citing its ability to melt solids and the way rays pass through each other. He introduces the analogy of sound waves traveling through air as a model for how light propagates.</summary>

<keywords>mechanical philosophy, nature of light, motion, concave mirrors, speed of light, sound analogy, propagation</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues Huygens' argument against the "corpuscular" theory of light (light as particles). Having dismissed the idea that light is a physical object traveling like an arrow, he now begins to develop his famous "wave theory" by comparing light to the way sound travels through the air.</meta>

->T R E A T I S E<-

from one part of the air to another, and that the spreading of this movement, happening with equal speed on all sides, must form something like spherical surfaces which continually expand and eventually strike our ear. Now, there is no doubt that light also reaches us from the luminous body by some movement imprinted on the matter that lies between the two; since we have already seen that it cannot be by the transport of a body that passes from one to the other. And if, in addition to that, light employs time for its passage—which we are now going to examine—it will follow that this movement imprinted on the matter is successive, and that consequently it spreads, just like that of Sound, by spherical surfaces and **waves**: for I call them **waves** <note>original: "ondes"</note> because of their resemblance to those one sees forming in water when a stone is thrown into it, which represent such a successive spreading in a circle, although they arise from a different cause and only on a flat surface.

To see then whether the spreading of light happens over time, let us first consider if there are any experiments that can convince us of the contrary. As for those that can be made here on Earth with fires placed at great distances, although they prove that light takes no perceptible time to cross these distances, one can rightly say that they are too short, and that one can only conclude that the passage of light is extremely fast. Mr. **Descartes** <note>René Descartes (1596–1650), a foundational figure in modern philosophy and science, who argued that light was an instantaneous pressure transmitted through a "plenum" or full universe.</note>, who was of the opinion that it is instantaneous, based his view, not without reason, on a much better experiment drawn from **Lunar Eclipses**; which, however, as I shall show, is not convincing. I will propose it a little differently than he did, to make its full consequence better understood.

Let **A** be the position of the sun, **B D** a part of the orbit or path

<meta>catchword: path</meta>

<summary>Huygens introduces his wave theory of light, comparing its propagation to ripples in water and sound in the air, while beginning to challenge Descartes' theory of instantaneous light.</summary>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues Huygens' discussion on the speed of light. Having introduced René Descartes' view that light might be instantaneous, Huygens now presents a geometric argument—often attributed to Descartes himself—used to support the idea that light must travel across space in an instant, rather than over time.</meta>

# OF LIGHT. CHAPTER I.

...annual path of the Earth. Let ABC be a straight line, which I suppose meets the path of the Moon, represented by the circle CD, at point C.

<image-desc>A geometric diagram illustrating astronomical principles. To the left is a point labeled A. A horizontal line extends from A to the right, passing through point B to reach point C on the far edge of a circle. The circle represents the path of the Moon and is labeled with points C, D, and G. A vertical-leaning arc DEB is drawn within the circle, intersecting the horizontal line at B. A diagonal line segment originates at A, passes through point E on the interior arc, and terminates at point G on the circle's perimeter. A line segment also connects points E and C.</image-desc>

Now, if light requires time—for example, one hour—to cross the space between the Earth and the Moon; it will follow that once the Earth has arrived at B, the shadow it causes, or the interruption of light, will not yet have reached point C, but will only arrive there one hour later. It will therefore be one hour later, counting from when the Earth was at B, that the Moon, arriving at C, will be darkened. But this darkening or interruption of light will not reach the Earth for another hour. Let us suppose that in these two hours, the Earth has reached E. The Earth then, being at E, will see the Moon eclipsed at C, from which the Moon departed an hour before, and will see the Sun at A at the same time. For, the Sun being immobile—as I suppose along with **Copernicus** <note>Nicolaus Copernicus (1473–1543) proposed the heliocentric model where the Earth revolves around the Sun, a theory Huygens defends here.</note>—and light extending in straight lines, it must always appear where it is. But it has always been observed, they say <note>Huygens is referring to the Cartesians, followers of Descartes, who argued that if light took time to travel, astronomical observations would be misaligned.</note>, that the eclipsed Moon appears at the point of the **Ecliptic** <note>The apparent path of the Sun across the celestial sphere; an eclipsed Moon is expected to be exactly 180 degrees opposite the Sun.</note> opposite the Sun; and yet here, it would appear behind that position by the angle GEC, the complement of AEC to two right angles <note>In 17th-century geometry, "two right angles" refers to a straight line of 180 degrees.</note>. Therefore, this is contrary to

<summary>Huygens uses a geometric diagram to explain an objection to the finite speed of light: if light took time to travel, the Earth would move during a lunar eclipse, causing the Moon to appear out of alignment with the Sun.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the geometric proof regarding the speed of light. Huygens is calculating the expected displacement (angle) of an eclipse if light traveled at a certain speed, comparing it to astronomical observations to see if the delay would be perceptible to observers on Earth.</meta>

experience, since the angle *G E C* would be quite noticeable, and around 33 degrees. For according to our calculation, which is in the *Treatise on the causes of the phenomena of Saturn* <note>original: "Traitté des cauſes des phenomenes de Saturne"; referring to Huygens' 1659 work *Systema Saturnium*.</note>, the distance *B A* between the Earth and the Sun is about twelve thousand Earth diameters, and consequently four hundred times greater than *B C*, the distance of the Moon, which is 30 diameters. Therefore, the angle *E C B* will be nearly four hundred times greater than *B A E*, which is five minutes; namely, the path the Earth travels in two hours in its orbit; and thus the angle *B C E* [is] nearly 33 degrees; and likewise the angle *C E G*, which exceeds it by five minutes.

But it must be noted that the speed of light in this reasoning has been set such that it requires one hour of time to travel from here to the Moon. If one supposes that it requires only one minute of time, then it is manifest that the angle *C E G* will be only 33 minutes, and if it requires only ten seconds of time, this angle will not be six minutes. And then it is not easy to perceive in observations of an Eclipse, nor consequently permitted to conclude anything from it regarding the instantaneous movement of light.

It is true that this is to suppose a strange speed which would be a hundred thousand times greater than that of Sound. For Sound, according to what I have observed, travels about 180 <term>Toises</term> <note>An old French unit of length equal to approximately 1.949 meters (roughly 6.4 feet).</note> in the time of a Second or of a heartbeat. But this supposition should not seem to have anything impossible about it; because it is not a matter of the transport of a body with such speed, but of a <term>successive movement</term> <note>Huygens distinguishes between a single object flying through space and a wave-like pulse passing through a medium, which he calls "successive movement."</note> which passes from one to the others. I therefore did not find it difficult, in meditating on these things, to suppose that the emanation of light occurred with time, seeing that through this all its phenomena could be explained, and that in following the contrary opinion everything was incomprehensible. For it has always seemed to me, and to many others with me, that even **Mr. Des Cartes** <note>René Descartes (1596–1650), the influential French philosopher and mathematician who argued that light was an instantaneous pressure rather than a movement over time.</note>, who aimed to treat things intelligi-

OF LIGHT. CHAPTER I.

<meta>catchword: half</meta>

...ment of all subjects of Physics, and who has certainly succeeded much better than anyone before him, has said nothing that is not full of difficulties, or even inconceivable, concerning Light and its properties.

But what I used only as a hypothesis has recently received the great appearance of a constant truth, through the ingenious demonstration of Mr. Romer <note>Ole Rømer (1644–1710), the Danish astronomer who first demonstrated that light has a finite speed.</note> which I shall report here, while waiting for him to provide everything that should serve to confirm it. It is founded, like the previous one, on celestial observations, and proves not only that light takes time for its passage, but also shows how much time it takes, and that its speed is at least six times greater than that which I have just mentioned.

<image-desc>A geometric diagram of the solar system illustrating the movement of a satellite around Jupiter. A central point 'A' represents the Sun, surrounded by a large circular 'annual orbit of the Earth' labeled with points B, C, D, and E. He makes use, for this purpose, of the Eclipses suffered by the small Planets that revolve around Jupiter, and which often enter into its shadow, and here is his reasoning. Let **A** be the sun, **BCDE** the annual orbit of the Earth, **F** Jupiter, **GN** the orbit of the nearest of its Satellites, for this one is more suitable for this research than any of the other three, because of the speed of its revolution. Let **G** be this Satellite entering the shadow of Jupiter, **H** the same exiting the shadow.

Supposing then that the Earth being at **B**, some time before the last quadrature <term>quadrature</term> <note>A position where the Sun and a celestial body are at a 90-degree angle as seen from Earth.</note>, one has seen the said Satellite emerge from the shadow; it would be necessary, if the Earth remained in this same place, that after 42 hours and a half

<meta>catchword: being</meta>

half, one would see another such emersion <note>The reappearance of a celestial body after being eclipsed by another.</note>; because that is the time in which it completes the circuit of its orbit and returns to opposition with the Sun. And if the Earth remained always at **B** during 30 revolutions, for example, of this Satellite, it would still see it exit the shadow after 30 times 42 and a half hours. But the Earth having moved during this time to **C**, moving further away from Jupiter, it follows that if light consumes time in its passage, the illumination of the small planet will be perceived later at **C** than it would have been at **B**; and that one must add to this time of 30 times 42 and a half hours also the time light takes to cross the space **MC**, which is the difference between the distances **CH** and **BH**. Similarly, towards the other quadrature <note>An astronomical position where two celestial bodies are 90 degrees apart as seen from Earth.</note>, when the Earth has come from **D** to **E** while approaching Jupiter, the immersions <note>The disappearance of a celestial body as it enters the shadow of another.</note> of the Satellite **G** into the shadow should be observed earlier at **E** than they would have appeared if the Earth had remained at **D**.

Now, by a great number of observations of these Eclipses made over ten consecutive years, these differences have been found to be very considerable—as much as ten minutes or more—and it has been concluded that to traverse the entire diameter of the annual orbit **KL**, which is twice the distance from here to the sun, light requires about 22 minutes of time.

The movement of Jupiter in its orbit, while the Earth passes from **B** to **C** or from **D** to **E**, is included in this calculation; and it is shown that the delay of these illuminations, nor the anticipation of the Eclipses, cannot be attributed to any irregularity in the movement of this small planet, nor to its eccentricity.

Now, if one considers the vast extent of the diameter **KL**, which in my view is some 24,000 Earth diameters <note>A remarkably accurate estimate for the late 17th century; Huygens was calculating the "Astronomical Unit," or the distance between the Earth and the Sun.</note>, one will recognize the extreme speed of light. For assuming that **KL** is only 22,000 of these diameters, it appears that being

...distance traveled in 22 minutes, this makes a thousand diameters <note>Referring to the diameter of the Earth.</note> in one minute, and 16 2/3 diameters in a second or "beat of an artery" <note>Before reliable stopwatches, scientists often used their own pulse to measure seconds.</note>, which equals more than eleven hundred times one hundred thousand toises <note>A toise is an old French unit of length, approximately 1.95 meters or 6.4 feet.</note>; since the diameter of the Earth contains 2865 leagues (at 25 leagues to a degree), and each league is 2282 toises, following the exact measurement that Mr. Picard <note>Jean Picard (1620–1682), a French astronomer who accurately measured the size of the Earth.</note> took by order of the King in 1669. Now, the successive movement of light being confirmed in this manner, it follows, as I have already said, that it spreads in spherical waves, just like the movement of Sound.

But if both resemble each other in this regard, they differ in several other things; namely, in the first production of the movement that causes them; in the matter through which the movement spreads; and in the manner in which it is communicated. For as far as the production of Sound is concerned, it is known that it occurs by the sudden vibration of an entire body, or a considerable part of it, which agitates all the contiguous air. But the movement of light must originate as if from every point of the luminous object, so that all the different parts of this object can be perceived, as will be seen more clearly later on. And I do not believe that this movement can be better explained than by supposing those luminous bodies that are liquid—such as flame, and apparently the sun and the stars—to be composed of particles swimming in a much more subtle matter, which agitates them with great rapidity and causes them to strike against the particles of the ether <note>A hypothetical substance once thought to fill all space and serve as the medium for light waves.</note> surrounding them, which are much smaller than they are. But in solid luminous bodies, such as charcoal or metal heated red-hot, this same

<meta>This page continues from the discussion on the previous page where the author compared the speed of light to the speed of sound, concluding that while light is incredibly fast, it is not instantaneous. ->TREATISE<-

movement is caused by the violent vibration of the particles of metal or wood, whose surface particles strike the **ethereal matter** in the same way. Furthermore, the agitation of the particles that generate light must be much quicker and faster than that of the bodies that cause sound; for we do not see that the quivering of a sounding body is capable of giving birth to light, just as the movement of a hand in the air is not capable of producing Sound.

Now, if one examines what this matter could be in which the movement coming from luminous bodies spreads—which I call **Ethereal**—one will see that it is not the same matter that serves the propagation of Sound. For we find that the latter is properly this air that we feel and that we breathe; when this is removed from a place, the other matter that serves light does not fail to be there. This is proven by enclosing a sounding body in a glass vessel, from which the air is then drawn out by the machine that <term>Mr. Boyle</term> <note>Robert Boyle (1627–1691), a pioneering chemist and physicist. He developed an improved air pump to study the properties of vacuums.</note> has given us, and with which he has performed so many beautiful experiments. But in performing the experiment of which I speak, one must take care to place the sounding body on cotton or on feathers, so that it cannot communicate its vibrations to the glass vessel that encloses it, nor to the machine—a detail which had until now been neglected. For then, after having emptied all the air, one does not hear the Sound of the metal at all, even though it is struck.

From this, we see not only that our air, which does not penetrate glass, is the matter through which Sound spreads; but also that it is not this same air, but another matter, in which light spreads; since, once the air is removed from this vessel, light does not fail to pass through it as before.

### ON LIGHT. CHAPTER I.
<sig>B 2</sig>

the famous experiment of <term>Torricelli</term> <note>Evangelista Torricelli (1608–1647) was an Italian physicist who invented the barometer. His experiment proved that a vacuum could exist by inverting a tube of mercury.</note>; where the glass tube, from which the <term>quicksilver</term> <note>original: "vif argent," the historical term for mercury.</note> has withdrawn, remaining completely void of air, transmits light in the same way as when there is air: for this proves that a matter different from air is found in this tube, and that this matter must have pierced the glass, or the quicksilver, or both, which are both impenetrable to air. And when in the same experiment a vacuum is made by putting a little water over the quicksilver, one similarly concludes that the said matter passes through the glass, or the water, or through both.

As for the different ways in which I said the movements of **Sound** and of **light** are successively communicated, one can understand well enough how this happens regarding **Sound**, when one considers that air is of such a nature that it can be compressed, and reduced to a space much smaller than it usually occupies; and that as it is compressed it makes an effort to expand again: for this, combined with its penetrability, which remains despite its compression, seems to prove that it is made of small bodies that swim and are agitated very quickly in the **ethereal matter**, composed of much smaller parts. So that the cause of the spreading of **sound waves** is the effort made by these small bodies, which strike one another, to expand again when they are a bit more squeezed in the circuit of these waves than elsewhere.

But the extreme speed of light, and other properties it has, could not admit such a propagation of movement, and I will show here in what manner I conceive it must be. For that, it is necessary to explain the property that **hard bodies** possess in transmitting movement to one another.

When one takes a number of balls of equal size, made of some very hard material, and arranges them in a line

<meta>This page continues Christian Huygens' explanation of how movement is communicated through a medium. On the previous page, he compared the movement of sound through air to the movement of light through an ethereal matter. Here, he uses the analogy of colliding spheres—similar to a modern Newton's Cradle—to demonstrate that movement travels successively through solid bodies, which serves as a model for how light travels through the "ether."</meta>

<page-num>12</page-num>

straight, in such a way that they touch each other; one finds, by striking with a similar ball against the first of these balls, that the movement passes as if in an instant to the last one, which separates from the row, without one perceiving that the others have moved. And even the one that struck remains motionless with them. Wherein one sees a passage of movement of extreme speed, and which is all the greater as the material of the balls is of a greater hardness.

But it is also certain that this progress of movement is not instantaneous, but successive, and thus it requires time. For if the movement—or, if you will, the inclination to movement—did not pass successively through all these balls, they would all acquire it at the same time, and consequently they would all move forward together; which does not happen: but the last one leaves the whole row, and acquires the speed of the one that was pushed. Moreover, there are experiments that show that all these bodies which we rank among the hardest, such as tempered steel, glass, and Agate, possess <term>elasticity</term> <note>original: "font ressort," literally "act as a spring." Huygens is describing the physical property where a material deforms under pressure and then returns to its shape.</note>, and bend in some way, not only when they are stretched out into rods, but also when they are in the form of balls or otherwise. That is to say, they compress slightly into themselves at the spot where they are struck, and immediately return to their original shape. For I have found that by striking with a glass or Agate ball against a large and very thick piece of the same material, which had a flat surface that was slightly tarnished with breath or otherwise, round marks remained there, larger or smaller depending on whether the blow was strong or weak. This shows that these materials yield upon impact and restore themselves; a process for which they must employ time.

particles of the ether to be of a material so approaching perfect hardness and of a spring <note>original: "ressort." In this context, it refers to elasticity—the ability of a substance to instantly snap back to its original shape after being compressed.</note> so prompt as we desire. It is not necessary here to examine the cause of this hardness, nor that of the spring, the consideration of which would lead us too far from our subject. I will say, however, in passing, that one can conceive that these particles of the ether, notwithstanding their smallness, are still composed of other parts, and that their spring consists in the very rapid movement of a **subtle matter** <note>A 17th-century scientific concept of an extremely fine, fluid-like substance that fills all space and the gaps between larger particles.</note>, which traverses them from all sides, and constrains their texture to arrange itself in such a way that it gives a passage to this fluid matter that is as open and as easy as possible. This accords with the reason that ***Mr. Des Cartes*** <note>René Descartes (1596–1650), the famous French philosopher and mathematician whose mechanical theories of the universe Huygens is both using and refining.</note> gives for the spring, except that I do not suppose pores in the form of round, hollow channels as he does. And one must not imagine that there is anything absurd in this nor anything impossible, it being on the contrary quite believable that it is this infinite progression of different sizes of **corpuscles** <note>Small particles or atoms.</note>, and the different degrees of their speed, which ***Nature*** uses to operate so many marvelous effects.

But even if we were ignorant of the true cause of the spring, we always see that there are many bodies which have this property; and thus there is nothing strange in supposing it also in small invisible bodies like those of the ***Ether***. Furthermore, if one wishes to seek some other way in which the movement of light is communicated successively, one will find none that fits better than the spring with the equal progression that seems to be necessary; because if this movement slowed down as it was shared among more matter, in moving away from the source of the light, it would not be able to preserve this great speed over great distances. But by supposing the spring in the ethereal matter, its particles will have the property of restoring themselves equally fast, whether they are strongly or weakly pushed; and thus the progress of light [will continue...]

I have therefore shown in what way one can conceive that light extends successively by spherical waves, and how it is possible that this extension occurs with such a great speed as experiments and celestial observations require. Here it must also be noted that although the particles of the **ether** <note>The "ether" was a hypothetical substance that 17th-century scientists believed filled all space to allow light waves to travel, much like air allows sound to travel.</note> are supposed to be in continual motion (for there are many reasons for this), the successive propagation of the waves cannot be hindered by it, because it does not consist in the transport of these particles, but only in a small <term>disturbance: a localized vibration or tremor (original: "ébranlement")</term>, which they cannot help but communicate to those surrounding them, despite all the motion that agitates them and makes them change places among themselves.

Furthermore, it should not seem inconceivable that this prodigious quantity of
waves, which cross each other without confusion or erasing one
another, is possible; it being certain that the same particle of matter can serve several waves, coming from different sides, or even from opposite sides; not only if it is pushed by strokes that follow closely one after another, but even by those acting upon it at the same instant; and this is because of the movement which extends successively. This can be proven by the row of equal balls, made of hard matter, of which we spoke above; against which, if one pushes similar balls *A* and *D* at the same time from the two opposite sides, one will see each rebound with the same speed it had when moving forward, and the entire row remain in its place; although the movement has passed all along its length, and doubly so. And if these contrary movements happen to meet at the middle ball *B*, or at some other like *C*, it must bend and act as a **spring** <term>spring: the property of elasticity (original: "ressort") where a material compressed by force immediately seeks to return to its original shape, pushing the next object</term> from both sides, and thus serve in the same instant to transmit these two movements.

<image-desc>A scientific diagram showing the linear arrangement of seven spherical balls. On the far left, a ball labeled 'A' is separated by a gap from a central cluster of five touching balls. The middle ball of this cluster is labeled 'B', and the ball to its immediate right is labeled 'C'. To the right of the central cluster, separated by another gap, is a final ball labeled 'D'. This illustrates the transmission of kinetic energy through a medium.</image-desc>

But what may at first appear very strange and even in-
credible, is that undulations produced by move-
ments and corpuscles so small, can extend to dis-
tances so immense, as for example from the sun,
or from the stars all the way to us. For the force of these
waves must weaken as they spread out from their origin,
so that the action of each one in particular will doubt-
less become incapable of being felt by our sight. But one will
cease to be astonished by considering that at a great distance

### OF LIGHT. CHAPTER I.

...from the luminous body an infinity of waves, though originating from different points of this body, unite in such a way that they perceptibly compose only a single wave, which consequently must have enough strength to be felt. Thus, this infinite number of waves which arise at the same instant from all the points of a fixed star, perhaps as large as the Sun, perceptibly form only a single wave, which may well have enough strength to make an impression on our eyes. Furthermore, from each luminous point, several thousand waves can come in the shortest imaginable time, through the frequent percussion <note>the impact or striking of one body against another</note> of the **corpuscles** <term>corpuscles: the tiny, invisible particles that 17th-century scientists believed made up all matter</term> which strike the **Ether** <term>Ether: a theoretical substance once thought to fill all space and allow light to travel</term> at these points; this contributes even more to making their action more sensible <note>perceptible to the senses</note>.

There is also to be considered in the emanation of these waves, that each particle of the matter in which a wave extends must not communicate its movement only to the next particle that lies in the straight line drawn from the luminous point; but that it also necessarily gives movement to all others that touch it and which oppose its motion.

<image-desc>A geometric diagram illustrating Christiaan Huygens' principle of wave propagation. A primary wave source is at point 'A' (the apex of a triangle). An arc 'D C F' represents a wave front. Along an intermediate path, points 'B' and 'G' (marked with small 'b's) act as secondary sources, generating smaller hemispherical wavelets (marked with 'd's). One specific wavelet 'K C L' originating from point 'B' is shown touching the primary wave front 'D C F' at point 'C'.</image-desc>

So that it is necessary that around each particle a wave is formed, of which this particle is the center. Thus, if **D C F** is a wave emanating from the luminous point **A**, which is its center; the particle **B**, one of those comprised within the sphere **D C F**, will have made its particular wave **K C L**, which will touch the wave **D C F** in **C**, at the same moment that the principal wave, emanating from point **A**, has reached—

# TREATISE

...extended into **D C F**; and it is clear that only the spot **c** of the wave **K C L** will touch the wave **D C F**, specifically the one located on the straight line drawn through **A B**. Likewise, the other particles contained within the sphere **D C F**, such as **b b**, **d d**, etc., will each have created its own wave. But each of these waves can only be infinitely weak compared to the wave **D C F**, to the composition of which all the others contribute through the part of their surface that is furthest from the center **A**.

Furthermore, one sees that the wave **D C F** is determined by the limit of the movement which has traveled from point **A** in a certain amount of time; there being no movement beyond this wave, although there is certainly some in the space it encloses—namely, in the parts of the individual waves which do not touch the sphere **D C F**. And all this should not seem to be researched with too much care or subtlety; since it will be seen in what follows that all the properties of light, and everything pertaining to its **reflection** <term>reflection: the process where light bounces off a surface, like a mirror</term> and its **refraction** <term>refraction: the bending of light as it passes from one substance into another, like from air into water</term>, are explained principally by this means. 

This is what was not known to those who previously began to consider waves of light, among whom are Mr. Hooke <note>Robert Hooke (1635–1703), an English scientist who published "Micrographia" in 1665</note> in his *Micrographia*, and Father Pardies <note>Ignace-Gaston Pardies (1636–1673), a French Jesuit whose work on optics influenced Huygens</note>, who in a treatise of which he showed me a part—and which he could not finish, having died shortly after—had undertaken to prove by these waves the effects of reflection and refraction. But the principal foundation, which consists of the observation I have just made, was missing from his demonstrations, and for the rest, he held opinions very different from mine, as perhaps will be seen one day if his writing has been preserved.

To come to the properties of light, let us first remark that each part of a wave must extend in such a way that the extremities are always contained between the same straight lines drawn from the luminous point. Thus, the part of the wave

Now, however small we make the opening **BG**, the reason remains the same for making light pass through it in straight lines; because this opening is always large enough to contain a great number of particles of ethereal matter <term>ethereal matter: an invisible, fluid-like substance once thought to fill all of space and carry light waves</term>, which are of an inconceivable smallness; so that it appears that each small portion of a wave necessarily advances along the straight line originating from the shining point. it is in this way that one can take rays of light as if they were straight lines.

It appears, moreover, from what has been noted regarding the weakness of the particular waves <note>Huygens refers to these as "secondary wavelets," the tiny ripples that originate from every point on a wavefront</note>, that it is not necessary for all the particles of the Ether <term>Ether: the hypothetical, invisible medium that scientists of this era believed filled all of space to allow the propagation of light waves</term> to be equal to one another, although equality would be most suitable for the propagation of movement. For it is true that inequality will cause a particle, upon pushing a larger one, to recoil with a part of its movement; however, this will only generate a few particular waves backward toward the luminous point, which are incapable of creating light—unlike the wave composed of many others, such as **CE** was.

Another of the most marvelous properties of light is that when it comes from different sides, or even from opposite ones, the rays produce their effect right through one another without any hindrance. This is also why, through a single opening, several spectators can see different objects all at once, and why two people can see into each other's eyes at the same instant. Now, according to what has been explained regarding the action of light—and how its waves are neither destroyed nor interrupted by one another when they cross—these effects I have just mentioned are easy to conceive. They are, in my opinion, not at all easy to understand according to the view of Des-Cartes <note>René Descartes (1596–1650), a famous philosopher and mathematician whose theory of light Huygens is challenging here</note>, who holds that light consists of a continual pressure <note>original: "pression continuelle"</note> that merely tends toward movement. For since this pressure cannot act all at once from two opposite sides against bodies that have no inclination to approach one another, it is impossible to understand what I have just said about two people seeing each other's eyes mutually, nor how two torches can illuminate one another.

### T R A I T É

Point **c** of wave **a c**, in a certain span of time, will have moved to point **b** on the plane **a b**, following the straight line **c b**. We must imagine this line as originating from the luminous center, and it is consequently perpendicular to the wave **a c**. Now, during this same span of time, point **a** of that same wave—having been prevented from communicating its motion beyond plane **a b**, or at least partially so—must have continued its movement within the matter that is above this plane. This movement covers a distance equal to **c b**, creating its own particular spherical wave <note>This is the essence of "Huygens' Principle": every point on a wavefront acts as a new source of secondary wavelets.</note>, according to what was stated above. This wave is represented here by the circumference **s n r**, whose center is **a**, and whose radius <note>original: "demidiametre"</note> **a n** is equal to **c b**.

If we then consider the other points **h** of the wave **a c**, it appears that they will not only have arrived at the surface **a b** via the lines **h k** (parallel to **c b**), but furthermore, from the centers **k**, they will have generated individual spherical waves within the <term>transparent medium</term> <note>original: "diaphane," referring to any substance like air or glass that allows light to pass.</note>. These are represented here by circles whose radii are equal to the lines **k m**—that is to say, to the continuations of the lines **h k** as far as the straight line **b g**, which is parallel to **a c**.

But all these circles have as a common tangent the straight line **b n**; specifically, the same line that is drawn from **b** as a tangent to the first of these circles, of which **a** was the center and **a n** the radius equal to **b c**, as is easy to see.

It is therefore the line **b n** (contained between **b** and the point **n**, where the perpendicular from point **a** falls) which is formed, as it were, by all these circumferences, and which terminates the movement produced by the reflection of the wave **a c**. It is also where this motion exists in much greater quantity than anywhere else. This is why, according to what has been explained, **b n** is the propagation <note>the forward-moving front</note> of the wave **a c** at the moment when its point **c** has arrived at **b**. For there is no other line which, like **b n**, is a common tangent to all the said circles, unless it be **b g** below the plane **a b**; which **b g** would be the propagation of the wave if

I maintain, then, that the wave **AC**, if we consider it as nothing more than a line, does not actually produce light. For any visible ray of light, no matter how slender it may be, always possesses some thickness; and therefore, to represent the wave whose progress forms this ray, one must, instead of a line **AC**, use a plane figure—such as the circle **HC** in the following figure—assuming, as we have done, that the source of light is infinitely distant. 

Now, it is easy to see, following the previous demonstration, that as each small part of this wave **HC** reaches the plane **AB** and generates its own particular wave <note>These "particular waves" are what we now call secondary wavelets, the foundational concept of Huygens' Principle.</note> from there; these will all have, when **C** has arrived at **B**, a common tangent plane <note>original: "commun plan qui les touchera"</note>, namely a circle **BN** similar to **CH**. ### ON LIGHT. CHAP. II.

...cir-cle **B N**; so that it will be this plane where there will be much more reflected movement than anywhere else, and which for that reason will carry the continued light of the wave **C H**.

I also said in the preceding demonstration that the movement of the area **A B** of the incident wave could not be communicated beyond the plane **A B**, or at least not entirely. Where it must be noted that, although the movement of the ethereal matter <note>original: "matiere etherée". This refers to the "luminiferous ether," a weightless substance that scientists in the 1600s believed filled all space to carry light waves, much like air carries sound.</note> was partially communicated to that of the reflecting body, this can in no way alter the speed of the progress of the waves, upon which the angle of reflection depends. For a slight percussion must generate waves as fast as a very strong one, in the same matter. This comes from the property of elastic bodies <term>corps qui font ressort: literally "bodies that act like springs," meaning elastic materials</term>, of which we have already spoken above; namely that whether they are pressed little or much, they restore themselves in equal times. Therefore, in every reflection of light, against any body whatsoever, the angles of reflection and incidence must be equal; notwithstanding that this body might be of such a nature that it removed part of the movement that makes up the incident light. And experience shows that, in effect, there is no polished body whose reflection does not follow this rule.

But what must be noted above all in our demonstration is that it does not require the reflecting surface to be considered as a smooth plane—as all those who have tried to explain the effects of reflection have supposed—but only of such an evenness as can be composed by the particles of the matter of the reflecting body, placed next to one another; which particles are larger than those of the ethereal matter, as will appear by what we shall say when treating the transparency and opacity of bodies. For with the surface consisting thus of particles joined together, and the ethereal particles being above them and smaller, it is evident that one could not demonstrate the equality of the angles of incidence, etc.

For the smallness of the particles of quicksilver <note>original: "vif argent," known today as mercury</note>, for example, being such that one must conceive of millions in the smallest visible surface proposed, arranged like a mass of grains of sand that have been leveled as much as one is capable of doing; this surface then becomes equal like a polished glass in our regard; and although it remains always rough in regard to the particles of the Ether <note>Huygens refers to the "luminiferous ether," the substance then believed to fill all space to carry light waves.</note>, it is evident that the centers of all the particular spheres of reflection, of which we have spoken, are nearly in the same level plane, and thus the common tangent <term>tangente: a line or plane that just touches the curve of the waves; here it represents the new wavefront</term> can suit them perfectly enough for what is needed for the production of light. And this is only what is required, in our manner of demonstrating, to make the equality of the said angles, without the rest of the reflected movement from all sides being able to produce any contrary effect.

## CHAPTER III.
### ON REFRACTION.

Just as the effects of Reflection have been explained by the waves of light reflected at the surface of polished bodies, we will explain transparency, and the phenomena of refraction, by the waves which extend within and through diaphanous bodies <term>diaphanes: transparent or translucent materials that allow light to pass through</term>, as much solids, like glass, as liquids, like water, oils, etc. But so that it does not appear strange to suppose this passage of waves inside these bodies, I will show beforehand that one can conceive it possible in more than one way.

First, then, even if the ethereal matter did not penetrate...

<meta>This page continues Chapter III, "On Refraction." The previous page ended while discussing how light waves pass through transparent liquids and solids. This page specifically addresses the physical composition of transparent materials like glass and how they transmit wave motion.</meta>

### ON LIGHT. CHAPTER III.
D 2
<meta>catchword: time;</meta>

in any way transparent bodies; their very particles could successively communicate the movement of waves, just like those of the Ether; being supposed, like the latter, to be of a nature to act as a spring <note>original: "faire reſſort"; this refers to particles having elasticity, allowing them to bounce back and transmit motion like a spring.</note>. And this is easy to conceive for water and other transparent liquids, as they are composed of detached particles. But it may seem more difficult regarding glass and other transparent and hard bodies, because their solidity does not seem to permit them to receive movement except in their entire mass at once. This, however, is not necessary, because this solidity is not as it appears to us; it being probable that these bodies are rather composed of particles which are merely placed next to one another, and held together by some external pressure from another matter, and by the irregularity of their shapes. For first, their rarity <term>rareté: the quality of being less dense or containing more internal space; the opposite of density</term> is apparent by the ease with which the matter of magnetic vortices <note>A reference to the Cartesian theory that magnetism was caused by the flow of invisible, swirling particles or "vortices."</note> and that which causes weight <note>gravity</note> passes through them. Furthermore, one cannot say these bodies are of a weave similar to that of a sponge or light bread, because the heat of fire makes them melt, and thereby changes the arrangement of the particles among themselves. It remains then that they are, as has been said, assemblies of particles that touch without composing a continuous solid. This being the case, the movement that these particles receive to continue the waves of light—only communicating it from one to another without being forced from their place or becoming disordered among themselves—can very well have its effect without prejudicing at all the solidity of the compound as it appears to us.

By the external pressure of which I spoke, one must not understand that of the air, which would not be sufficient, but another from a more subtle matter <note>Huygens refers to a highly fluid, invisible substance that fills the voids between larger particles of matter.</note>, which pressure reveals itself in an experiment that chance made me encounter a long—

<summary>Huygens argues that transparent solids like glass are not continuous blocks of matter but are made of discrete particles held together by external pressure, allowing light waves to pass through them like a spring-like motion without displacing the particles. He cites the movement of magnetic forces and gravity through solids as evidence of their internal gaps and "rarity."</summary>

<keywords>refraction, particle theory, elasticity, transparency, ether, magnetic vortices, subtle matter, physical structure of glass</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Chapter III, "On Refraction." On the previous page, the author was discussing how particles in solid bodies are held together and how they might transmit wave motion. This page concludes a point about suspended liquids and begins an argument for why "ethereal matter" must be able to pass through solid objects like glass.</meta>

->T R E A T I S E<-

time; namely water purged of air, which remains suspended in a glass tube open at the bottom, despite the air being removed from the vessel in which this tube is enclosed <note>This refers to a famous 17th-century experiment regarding atmospheric pressure and the vacuum; the "water purged of air" stays in the tube because of the lack of air bubbles that would normally allow the column to break.</note>.

One can therefore, in this manner, conceive of transparency without it being necessary for the ethereal matter, which serves to carry light, to pass through it, or for it to find pores to creep into. But the truth is that this matter not only passes through, but does so with great ease, for which the experiment of Torricelli <note>Evangelista Torricelli (1608–1647), the inventor of the barometer, who proved that the space above mercury in a sealed tube is a vacuum.</note>, cited above, is already a proof: because when the mercury <term>vif argent: literally "living silver," the archaic term for mercury</term> and the water leave the upper part of the glass tube, it appears that it is immediately filled with ethereal matter, since light passes through it. But here is another argument which proves this easy penetrability, not only in transparent bodies, but also in all others.

When light passes through a hollow sphere of glass, closed on all sides, it is certain that it is full of the ethereal matter, just as much as the spaces outside the sphere. And this ethereal matter, as has been shown previously, consists of particles that touch one another closely. If it were therefore so enclosed in the sphere that it could not exit through the pores of the glass, it would be obliged to follow the movement of the sphere when one makes it change place: and it would consequently require nearly the same force to impart a certain speed to this sphere, when it was placed on a horizontal plane, as if it were full of water or perhaps of mercury: because every body resists the speed of the movement one wishes to give it, according to the quantity of matter it contains, and which must follow that movement <note>This is an early description of the concept of inertia—the idea that the more "stuff" or mass an object has, the harder it is to push.</note>. But one finds, on the contrary, that the sphere only resists the impulse of movement according to the quantity of the glass matter of which it is made: therefore, it must be that the ethereal matter, which is inside, is not enclosed at all, but that it flows through with very great liberty.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "ethereal matter" (the medium for light) and its ability to penetrate solid objects. The previous page established that even in a vacuum (like that in a Torricellian tube), light passes through, implying the ether remains present. ### ON LIGHT. CHAP. III.

<margin>D 3</margin>

We shall show hereafter that the same penetrability is also inferred, by this means, in the case of opaque bodies. The second way, then, of explaining transparency, which appears more likely, is by saying that light waves continue through the ethereal matter <note>Huygens' "ether" was the invisible medium he believed light traveled through, similar to how air carries sound waves.</note> that continuously occupies the interstices, or pores, of transparent bodies. Since it passes through them continually and with ease, it follows that they are always found full of it. And one can even demonstrate that these interstices occupy much more space than the coherent particles that constitute the bodies. For if what we just said is true—that force is required to impart a certain horizontal speed to bodies in proportion to the amount of coherent matter <term>coherent matter: the solid, physical particles of an object as opposed to the empty space or ether between them</term> they contain; and if the proportion of this force follows the ratio of weights, as is confirmed by experience—then the quantity of matter constituting bodies also follows the proportion of their weights. Now we see that water weighs only one-fourteenth as much as an equal portion of mercury <term>vif argent: literally "living silver," the archaic term for mercury</term>: therefore the matter of water does not occupy even the fourteenth part of the space of its mass. Indeed, it must occupy much less, since mercury is less heavy than gold, and the matter of gold is very little dense; as follows from the fact that the matter of the magnet's vortices <note>A reference to the Cartesian theory that magnetism is caused by "vortices" or whirlpools of invisible particles flowing through the pores of a magnet.</note> and that which causes gravity pass through it very freely.

But one might object here that, if the body of water is of such great rarity <note>In 17th-century physics, "rarity" referred to low density or having many gaps between particles.</note>, and its particles occupy such a small portion of its apparent extent, it is very strange that it nonetheless resists compression so strongly, without allowing itself to be condensed by any force yet attempted; while even preserving all its liquidity while undergoing this pressure.

This is no small difficulty. Which, however, one

The rarity <note>In this context, "rarity" refers to the high proportion of empty space or pores within a substance relative to its solid matter.</note> of transparent bodies being such as we have described, one easily understands that waves can be continued in the ethereal matter which fills the interstices between the particles. Furthermore, one can believe that the progress of these waves must be a little slower inside the bodies, because of the small detours caused by these same particles. I will show that the cause of refraction <term>refraction: the bending of light as it passes from one substance into another, such as from air into glass</term> consists in this difference in the speed of light.

I will first point out the third and final way in which transparency can be conceived: by supposing that the movement of light waves is transmitted indifferently both through the particles of ethereal matter filling the interstices of bodies, and through the particles that compose the bodies themselves, such that this movement passes from one to the other. It will be seen hereafter that this hypothesis is very useful for explaining the double refraction <term>double refraction: a property of certain crystals, like Iceland spar, where a single ray of light is split into two separate rays</term> of certain diaphanous <note>diaphanous: another word for transparent or translucent</note> bodies.

If it is objected that since the particles of the ether are smaller than those of transparent bodies (since they pass through their gaps), it would follow that they could communicate only a little of their movement to them; one may answer that the particles of these bodies are themselves composed of other smaller particles, and thus it will be these secondary particles that receive the movement from the particles of the ether.

Furthermore, if the particles of transparent bodies have a spring <note>original: "ressort". Huygens uses this term to describe the elasticity or "recoil" of particles that allows them to transmit waves of movement.</note> slightly less prompt than that of the ethereal particles—which nothing prevents us from supposing—it will follow again that the progress of light waves will be slower inside these bodies than it is outside in the ethereal matter.

### ON LIGHT. CHAPTER III.

This is all that I have found to be most likely regarding the manner in which light waves pass through transparent bodies. To this, it must also be added how these bodies differ from those that are opaque; especially since it might seem—because of the easy penetration of bodies by the ethereal matter <term>ethereal matter: a hypothesized extremely fine substance that fills the voids of the universe and carries light waves</term> of which we have spoken—that there would be no body that was not transparent. For by the same reasoning of the hollow sphere, which I used to prove the low density of glass and its easy penetrability by ethereal matter, one can also prove that the same penetrability applies to metals and all other kinds of bodies. For if this sphere were made of silver, for example, it is certain that it contains ethereal matter which serves the propagation of light, since this matter was inside just as much as the air was when the opening of the sphere was closed. However, once closed and placed on a horizontal plane, it only resists the movement one wishes to give it according to the quantity of silver of which it is made. So, it must be concluded, as before, that the enclosed ethereal matter does not follow the movement of the sphere; and that consequently silver, as well as glass, is very easily penetrated by this matter. It is therefore found continuously and in quantity between the particles of silver and all other opaque bodies; and since this matter serves the propagation of light, it seems that these bodies should also be transparent, like glass; which, however, is not the case.

From where, then, shall it be said that their opacity comes? Is it because the particles that compose them are soft <note>In the physics of this era, "soft" or "plastic" particles would absorb energy by changing shape, whereas "hard" particles would transmit it elastically.</note>—that is to say, that these particles, being composed of other smaller ones, are capable of changing their shape upon receiving the impact of the ethereal particles, by which they deaden the movement and thus prevent the continuation of the light waves? That cannot be: for if the particles of metals are soft, how is it...

Let us now move to the explanation of the effects of **Refraction**; assuming, as we have done, the passage of light waves through transparent bodies, and the decrease in speed that these same waves suffer therein.

The principal property of **Refraction** is that a ray of light, such as **AB**, being in the air and falling obliquely upon the polished surface of a transparent body such as **FG**, breaks at the point of incidence **B**, so that with the straight line **DBE**, which cuts the surface perpendicularly, it makes an angle **CBE** smaller than **ABD**, which it made with the same perpendicular while in the air.

<meta>This page continues Christiaan Huygens' geometric demonstration of light propagation. Having previously established the law of refraction (Snell's Law) through the ratio of speeds in different media, he now addresses the limit of this phenomenon—where light is no longer refracted but is instead reflected entirely.</meta>

### ON LIGHT. CHAP. III.

One also sees the reason for a notable occurrence <note>Huygens is describing the phenomenon of "total internal reflection," where light hitting a boundary at a steep enough angle cannot pass through and is instead reflected back.</note> that happens in this refraction; which is that beyond a certain obliquity of the incident ray *DA*, it begins to be unable to penetrate into the other <term>diaphanous: a scientific term for a transparent substance or medium through which light can travel</term> body. For if the angle *DAQ* or *CBA* is such that in triangle *ACB*, *CB* is equal to $\frac{2}{3}$ of *AB*, or greater, then *AN* cannot form a side of triangle *ANB* because it becomes equal to *AB*, or greater: so that the part of the wave *BN* is found nowhere, nor consequently is *AN*, which ought to have been perpendicular to it. And thus the incident ray *DA* does not then pierce the surface *AB*.

When the ratio of the speeds of the waves is two to three, as in our example—which is the ratio that applies to glass and air—the angle *DAQ* must be greater than 48 degrees 11 minutes in order for the ray *DA* to be able to pass through by <term>refraction: the bending of a light ray when it passes from one medium, like air, into another, like glass</term>. And when the ratio of these speeds is 3 to 4, as it is very nearly in water and air, this angle *DAQ* must exceed 41 degrees 24 minutes. And this accords perfectly with experience.

But one might ask here, since the meeting of the wave *AC* against the surface *AB* must produce movement in the matter that is on the other side, why no light passes through there. To which the answer is easy if one remembers what was said previously. For although an infinity of particular waves <note>These are now known as "Huygens' wavelets." Huygens proposed that every point on a wavefront acts as a source for new, tiny spherical waves.</note> are generated in the matter on the other side of *AB*, it does not happen that these waves have a common tangent line (whether straight or curved) at the same instant; and thus there is no line that terminates the propagation of the wave *AC* beyond the plane *AB*, nor where the movement is gathered in great enough quantity to produce light. And one will easily see the truth of this—namely, that *CB* being greater than $\frac{2}{3}$ of *AB*, the waves excited beyond the plane *AB* will have no common tangent, if from the centers *K* one de- <note>original: "de-crit" — the text cuts off, likely continuing as "describes" or "draws" circles/arcs on the next page.</note>

   Now it is to be noted that, as soon as the angle *D A Q* is smaller than is necessary to allow the refracted <note>original: "rompu"; literally 'broken,' the standard 17th-century term for refraction</note> ray *D A* to pass into the other <term>diaphanous: a substance that allows light to pass through; transparent</term> medium, it is found that the internal reflection, which occurs at the surface *A B*, increases greatly in brightness, as is easy to test with a triangular prism: for which we can give this reason by our Theory. When the angle *D A Q* is still large enough to allow the ray *D A* to pass, it is evident that the light of the wave-part *A C* is gathered into a smaller area once it has reached *B N*. It also appears that the wave *B N* becomes smaller as the angle *C B A* or *D A Q* is made smaller; until, having been decreased to the limit noted a moment ago, this wave *B N* gathers itself entirely as if into a point. That is to say, when the section *C* of the wave *A C* has then arrived at *B*, the wave *B N*, which is the propagation of *A C*, is entirely reduced to that same point *B*; just as, when the section *H* had arrived at *K*, the part *A H* was entirely reduced to that same point *K*. This shows that as the wave *C A* comes to meet the surface *A B*, a great amount of movement occurs along this surface; which movement must also have spread inside the transparent body, and have greatly reinforced the individual <note>original: "particulieres"; these are the secondary wavelets in Huygens' Principle that combine to form a new wavefront</note> waves, which produce the internal reflection against the surface *A B*, following the laws of reflection explained earlier.

   And because a slight reduction in the angle of incidence *D A Q* makes the wave *B N* go from being quite large to nothing: (for when this angle in glass is 49 degrees 11 minutes, the angle *B A N* is still 11 degrees 21 minutes, and when the same angle *D A Q* is decreased by only one degree, the angle *B A N* is re- <meta>catchword: duced</meta>

<summary>Huygens explains that as the angle of incidence approaches the limit for refraction, the wavefront contracts into a single point, concentrating its energy at the surface. # ON LIGHT. CHAPTER III.

...duced to nothing, and thus the wave <term>B N: a geometric representation of the wave front</term> is reduced to a point:) from this it follows that the internal reflection suddenly becomes bright from being obscure, as soon as the angle of incidence is such that it no longer allows passage for refraction.

Now as for ordinary external reflection—that is to say, that which happens when the angle of incidence <term>D A Q</term> is still large enough to allow the refracted ray to penetrate beyond the surface <term>A B</term>—this reflection must occur against the particles of the matter which touches the transparent body from the outside. And it is apparently against the particles of air and others, mixed among the <term>ethereal matter: an invisible, fluid-like substance that 17th-century scientists believed permeated all space to carry light waves</term>, and coarser than it. Just as, on the other hand, the external reflection of these bodies occurs against the particles that compose them, which are also larger than those of the ethereal matter, since the latter flows within their gaps. It is true that some difficulty remains in experiments where this internal reflection occurs without the particles of air being able to contribute, as in vessels or tubes from which the air has been removed <note>Huygens is referring to the vacuum experiments of his time, such as those by Boyle or Pascal, noting that light still reflects even when air is absent</note>.

Experience, moreover, teaches us that these two reflections are of nearly equal strength, and that in different transparent bodies, they are stronger as the refraction of these bodies is greater. Thus, it is clearly seen that the reflection of glass is stronger than that of water, and that of diamond stronger than that of glass.

I shall finish this theory of refraction by demonstrating a remarkable proposition which depends on it: namely, that a ray of light, to go from one point to another when these points are in different <term>diaphanous: substances that allow light to pass through, such as water or glass</term> media, refracts at the flat surface joining these two media in such a way that it employs the least time possible; exactly as happens in reflection against a flat surface. <term>Mr. Fermat: Pierre de Fermat (1601–1665), a French mathematician who proposed that light always travels the path that takes the least time</term> first proposed this property of refractions, holding, as we do, and directly
