<meta>This page continues the geometric proof of the Principle of Least Time. The author is mid-demonstration, using a diagram to show that the path taken by a refracted ray of light is the one that minimizes the time of travel between two points in different media.</meta>

### ON LIGHT. CHAPTER III.

->F<-

AB, FO parallel to AB; BH perpendicular to FO, and FG on BC.

Since the angle HBF is equal to PBA, and the angle BFG equal to QBC; it follows that the sine of angle HBF will also have the same ratio to the sine of BFG as the speed of light in <term>medium A: the first substance, like air</term> to its speed in <term>medium C: the second substance, like water or glass</term>. But these sines are the straight lines HF and BG, taking BF as the <term>radius: "demi-diametre" in the original</term> of a circle. Therefore, these lines HF and BG have between them the aforementioned ratio of speeds. And consequently, the time light takes to travel through HF, assuming the ray was OF, would be equal to the time taken through BG within medium C. 

But the time taken through AB is equal to the time through OH; therefore the time through OF is equal to the time through AB and BG combined. Furthermore, the time through FC is longer than through GC; therefore the time through OFC will be longer than through ABC. But AF is greater than OF, so the time through AFC will exceed the time through ABC by even more.

Now let us assume that the ray has come from A to C through AK and KC; the point of refraction K being closer to A than point B is; and let CN be perpendicular to BC, and KN parallel to BC; BM perpendicular to KN, and KL to BA.

Here BL and KM are the sines of the angles BKL and KBM, that is to say, of the angles PBA and QBC; and consequently, they are to each other as the speed of light in medium A is to the speed in medium C. Therefore the time taken through LB is equal to the time through KM; and since the time through BC is equal to the time through MN, the time through LBC will be equal to the time through KMN. But the time through AK is longer than through AL; therefore the time through AKN is longer than through ABC. And since KC is longer than KN, the time through AKC will surpass the time through ABC by even more.

Thus it appears that the time along path ABC is the shortest it can be: <note>The classic "Quod Erat Demonstrandum" (Which was to be demonstrated).</note> which was to be proven.

<summary>This page concludes a geometric proof showing that the law of refraction (Snell's Law) results in light taking the path of "least time" between two points. By comparing various potential paths, the author demonstrates that any deviation from the refracted path increases the travel time.</summary>

# TREATISE

## CHAPTER IV.

### ON THE REFRACTION OF THE AIR.

We have shown how the movement that creates light spreads out in spherical waves within a <term>homogeneous: a substance that is uniform in composition and properties throughout</term> medium. It is evident that when the matter is not homogeneous, but of such a constitution that the movement is communicated faster toward one side than toward another, these waves cannot be spherical; instead, they must take their shape according to the different spaces that the successive movement traverses in equal increments of time.

It is by this principle that we shall first explain the refractions that occur in the air, which extends from here to the clouds and beyond. The effects of these refractions are quite remarkable; for it is through them that we often see objects that the <term>curvature: "rondeur" in the original, referring to the Earth's spherical shape</term> of the Earth would otherwise hide from us, such as islands and mountain peaks when one is at sea. Because of them, the Sun and the Moon also appear to have risen before they actually have, and to set later; so much so that the Moon has often been seen eclipsed while the Sun still appeared above the horizon. <note>This refers to a "horizontal eclipse," where atmospheric refraction allows both the eclipsed Moon and the Sun to be visible simultaneously, even though they are technically 180 degrees apart.</note> Thus, the heights of the Sun and Moon, and those of all the stars, always appear slightly greater due to these same refractions than they truly are, as astronomers well know. But there is an experiment that makes this refraction very visible: if one fixes a <term>telescope: "lunette d’approche," literally an "approach-glass"</term> in a certain place so that it points toward an object half a league away or more, such as a steeple or a house, and if one looks through it at different hours—

<meta>catchword: —tes</meta>

<summary>This page introduces atmospheric refraction, explaining that light waves change shape when traveling through non-homogeneous media like the air. This phenomenon allows observers to see celestial bodies and distant landmarks that would otherwise be hidden by the Earth's curvature.</summary>
<keywords>atmospheric refraction, wave theory, spherical waves, homogeneous media, astronomy, telescope, horizon, light movement</keywords>

Those who only consider refraction at the surfaces that separate transparent bodies of different natures would have difficulty accounting for everything I have just reported; but according to our Theory, the matter is quite easy. We know that the air surrounding us—besides the particles proper to it, which float in the <term>ethereal matter: the "aether," a weightless substance Huygens believed filled all space and transmitted light waves</term>, as has been explained—also fills with water particles raised by the action of heat; and it has been recognized, moreover, by very certain experiments, that the density of the air decreases as one climbs higher. Now, whether the particles of water and those of air participate—by means of the particles of ethereal matter—in the movement that creates light, but have a less rapid <term>spring: "ressort," the elastic property that allows particles to transmit motion</term> than the latter; or whether the encounter and the obstacle that these parts of air and water present to the <term>propagation: the spreading or transmission</term> of the movement of the ethereal particles delay its progress; it follows that both, flying among the ethereal particles, must make the air—from a great height down to the Earth—gradually less easy for the spreading of light waves.

From this, the <unclear>figure</unclear> <note>original: "figure"; partially obscured by an ink blot</note> of the waves must become approximately as this figure represents it. Namely, if A is a light, or a visible point of a steeple, the waves that arise from it must—

And this very fact is the reason why, at certain times, a distant object will be hidden behind another less distant one, and yet it may be seen at another time, even though the vantage point remains the same. But the reason for this effect will be even more evident from what we are about to observe regarding the **curvature of rays**. It appears from the things explained above that the progress, or the propagation of a particle of a light wave, is properly what we call a <term>ray: a line representing the path along which light energy travels</term>. Now these rays, instead of being straight as they are in <term>homogeneous transparent media: substances like glass or water that have the same composition throughout</term>, must be curved in air of unequal penetrability <note>original: "inegale penetrabilité," referring here to the varying density of the atmosphere</note>. For they necessarily follow the line which, from the object to the eye, intersects all the wave progressions at right angles, just as line **A E B** does in the first figure, as will be shown hereafter; and it is this line that determines which interposed bodies must prevent us from seeing the object or not. For although the tip of the steeple **A** appears elevated at **D**, it would nevertheless not be visible to the eye **B** if tower **H** were between the two, because it would cross the curve **A E B**. But tower **E**, which is below this curve, does not prevent point **A** from being seen. Now, depending on how much the air near the Earth exceeds in density that which is higher up, the curvature of ray **A E B** becomes greater; so that at certain times it passes above the summit **E**, causing point **A** to be perceived by the eye at **B**; and at other times it is interrupted by that same tower **E**, which hides **A** from the same eye.

## CHAPTER V.

### ON THE STRANGE REFRACTION OF ICELAND CRYSTAL.

I. There is brought from Iceland, which is an Island in the Northern Sea, at a latitude of 66 degrees, a type of Crystal, or transparent stone <note>original: "Cristal, ou pierre transparente"</note>, very remarkable for its shape and other qualities, but above all for that of its strange refractions. The causes of which seemed to me all the more worthy of being carefully researched, because among <term>diaphanous bodies: substances that allow light to pass through; transparent materials</term> this one alone, with regard to the rays of light, does not follow the ordinary rules. I even had a certain necessity to undertake this research, because the refractions of this Crystal <note>Now known as Iceland Spar or Calcite, famous for "double refraction"</note> seemed to overturn our preceding explanation of regular refraction.

<summary>This page concludes the proof that light rays curve as they pass through atmospheric layers of varying density and introduces the famous study of Iceland Spar, a crystal that defies standard laws of optics.</summary>
<keywords>atmospheric refraction, wave theory, Huygens' Principle, Iceland Spar, calcite, diaphanous bodies, light propagation</keywords>

--- Page 61 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction of "Iceland Crystal" (calcite), following the start of Chapter V. It describes the physical sources of the mineral, its discovery by Erasmus Bartholin, and begins a detailed geometric analysis of its unique shape and properties.</meta>

->OF LIGHT. CHAPTER V.<-

singular [phenomenon]; which, on the contrary, will be seen to be greatly confirmed after being reduced to the same principle. It is in Iceland that one finds large pieces of this Crystal, of which I have seen some weighing 4 or 5 pounds. But it also grows in other countries: for I have had some of the same species that was found in France near the city of Troyes in Champagne, and others that came from the Island of Corsica, although both were less clear and only in small pieces, barely capable of making any effect of refraction noticeable.

2. The first public knowledge of it is due to <term>Mr. Erasmus Bartholin: a Danish scientist (1625–1698) who first described the double refraction of Iceland spar</term>, who gave a description of Iceland crystal along with that of its principal phenomena. But I shall not fail to give my own here, as much for the instruction of those who will not have seen his book, as because in some of these phenomena there is a little difference between his observations and those I have made: having applied myself with great accuracy to examine these properties of refraction, in order to be quite certain before undertaking to clarify their causes.

3. If one considers the hardness of this stone, and its quality of being easily split, it should rather be considered a type of <term>Talc: a soft, flaky mineral; here Huygens uses it to categorize the crystal's physical texture</term>, than <term>Crystal: usually referring to quartz or harder minerals</term>. For an iron point scratches it as easily as other Talc, or as <term>Alabaster: a soft sulfate or carbonate mineral used for carving</term>, whose weight it equals.

4. The pieces found there are of the shape of an <term>oblique parallelepiped: a six-sided solid where each face is a parallelogram, but not necessarily at right angles</term>; each of the six faces being a parallelogram; and it allows itself to be split along all three dimensions, parallel to two of these opposite faces. Even so much so, if one wishes, that all six faces may be equal and similar <term>rhombi: equilateral parallelograms</term>. The figure added here <note>The figure mentioned is likely on the following page or in the margin of the original manuscript</note> represents a piece of this Crystal. Its transparency is hardly less than that of water or rock Crystal, and it is without any color. But rays of light pass through it in another way, and produce those marvelous <term>refractions: the bending of light as it passes from one medium to another</term>, the causes of which I shall now attempt to explain; deferring to the end of this Treatise the sharing of my conjectures regarding the formation and the extraordinary shape of this Crystal.

# OF LIGHT. CHAPTER V.

->G 2<-

§ 1. ...in this [crystal] there are two different [refractions]. This causes the objects seen through it, especially those placed directly against it, to appear double; and a ray of sun, falling upon one of its surfaces, splits in two as it traverses the Crystal.

7. It is also a general law in all other transparent bodies that a ray falling perpendicularly on their surface passes straight through without undergoing refraction; and that an oblique ray is always refracted <note>original: "se rompt" — literally 'breaks,' the 17th-century term for the bending of light</note>. But in this Crystal, the perpendicular ray undergoes refraction, and there are certain oblique rays that pass straight through.

8. To explain these phenomena more specifically, let us again take a piece of the same Crystal, **A B F E**, and let it be divided—

12. The method for making these observations exactly is as follows. One must draw upon a paper, fixed to a very smooth table, a <term>black line A B</term>, and two others which intersect it at right angles, <term>C E D, K M L</term>, more or less distant from one another according to whether one wishes to examine a ray that is more or less oblique: and place the <term>crystal</term> upon the <term>intersection E</term>, so that the line A B corresponds to the line which equally divides the <term>obtuse angle</term> <note>An angle greater than 90 degrees; here referring to the blunt corners of the diamond-shaped calcite crystal.</note> of the bottom surface, or to some parallel line. Then, by placing the <term>eye</term> directly above the line A B, it will appear only as a single line, and one will see that the part seen through the crystal meets the parts appearing outside in a straight line; but the line <term>C D</term> will appear double. One can distinguish the image coming from the <term>regular refraction</term> <note>Also called "ordinary" refraction; it follows the standard laws of optics where the light stays in the same plane.</note> because it appears more elevated than the other when looking with both eyes, or else because, when turning the crystal upon the paper, it remains still, while the other image moves and rotates all around it. One should then place the eye at <term>I</term> (remaining always in the perpendicular plane passing through A B) so that it sees the image of the line C D, which comes from the regular refraction, forming a straight line with the rest of that line which is outside the crystal. And marking then upon the surface of the crystal the <term>point H</term>, where the intersection E appears, this point will be directly above E. <meta>This page continues Huygens' transition from experimental observation of Iceland spar (calcite) to his theoretical explanation. Having established that the "extraordinary" ray does not follow standard laws, he now introduces his landmark theory of wave propagation—specifically his proposal that light can move in different shapes depending on the medium.</meta>

# T R E A T I S E

still other particular properties, we will seek the causes of the phenomena that I have already reported.

It was after having explained the refraction of ordinary transparent bodies, by means of the <term>spherical emanations</term> <note>Huygens' theory that light spreads out as a series of growing spheres, like ripples in a pond.</note> of light, as stated above, that I resumed the examination of the nature of this Crystal, where I had previously been unable to discover anything.

18. Since there were two different refractions, I conceived that there were also two different emanations of <term>light waves</term>, and that one could be produced in the <term>ethereal matter</term> <note>A hypothetical substance (aether) believed by 17th-century scientists to fill all space and allow light to travel, much like air allows sound to travel.</note> spread throughout the body of the crystal. This matter, being in much greater quantity than that of the particles which compose it, was alone capable of causing transparency, according to what has been explained previously. I attributed to this emanation of waves the regular refraction observed in this stone; assuming these waves to be of the usual spherical shape, and of a slower expansion inside the crystal than they are outside: from which I have shown that refraction proceeds.

19. As for the other emanation which was to produce the <term>irregular refraction</term>, I wanted to try what <term>Elliptical waves</term>—or to speak more accurately, **spheroids**—would do. I assumed that these extended indifferently, both in the ethereal matter spread through the crystal and in the particles of which it is composed; following the latter manner in which I explained transparency. It seemed to me that the disposition, or regular arrangement of these particles, could contribute to forming the spheroidal waves (it being required for this only that the <term>successive movement</term> of light spread a little faster in one direction than in the other), and I had almost no doubt that there was in this crystal such an arrangement of equal and similar particles, because of its shape and its angles of a certain measure...

<summary>Huygens proposes his revolutionary theory that light moves through crystals in two different ways: as traditional spherical waves (regular refraction) and as "spheroidal" (oval) waves caused by the internal structure of the crystal (irregular refraction).</summary>
<keywords>wave theory, light waves, ethereal matter, aether, spherical waves, spheroids, double refraction, Iceland spar, crystal structure</keywords>

certain and invariable. Concerning these particles, and their shape and arrangement, I will propose toward the end of this Treatise my conjectures, and some experiments which confirm them.

20. The double emanation of light waves that I had imagined became more probable to me after a certain phenomenon I observed in ordinary crystal <note>Huygens is referring to rock crystal or quartz.</note> which grows in a hexagonal shape, and which, because of this regularity, also seems to be composed of particles of a certain shape and arranged in an orderly fashion. This was because this crystal has a **double refraction**, just like Iceland spar, though less obvious. For having had well-polished Prisms cut by different sections, I noticed in all of them—when looking at a candle flame through them, or at the lead of the windowpanes—that everything appeared double, though with images not very far apart. From this I understood the reason why this body, otherwise so transparent, is useless for <term>telescopes</term> <note>original: "Lunettes d’approche"</note> when they are of any significant length.

21. Now, this double refraction, following my Theory established above, seemed to demand a double emanation of light waves, both of them spherical (for both refractions are regular) and with one only a little slower than the other. For by this, the phenomenon is explained quite naturally, by assuming the substances which serve as the <term>vehicle</term> <note>The medium through which the waves travel.</note> for these waves to be the same as those I used for the Iceland crystal. I thus had less trouble after that admitting two emanations of waves within a single body. And because one could object to me that in composing these two crystals of equal particles of a certain shape, regularly stacked, the <term>interstices</term> <note>The tiny gaps or spaces between the particles.</note> that these particles leave—which contain the <term>ethereal matter</term>—would hardly suffice to transmit the light waves I have placed there, I removed this difficulty by considering these particles as being of a very <term>porous</term> <note>original: "rare," meaning low density or sparse structure.</note> texture, or else composed of o—

->T R E A T I S E<-

...other much smaller particles, between which the **ethereal matter** <term>ethereal matter</term> <note>Also known as "aether," this was a substance scientists once believed filled all space to carry light waves, similar to how air carries sound.</note> passes very freely. This, moreover, necessarily follows from what was demonstrated previously regarding the small amount of actual matter of which bodies are composed.

22. Assuming then these **spheroidal waves** <note>Unlike a perfectly round spherical wave, a spheroidal wave is shaped like an oval or ellipsoid.</note> in addition to the spherical ones, I began to examine whether they could serve to explain the phenomena of **irregular refraction**, and how, by these very phenomena, I might determine the shape and position of the spheroids. In this, I finally obtained the desired success by proceeding as follows.

23. I first considered the effect of the waves thus formed with regard to the **ray** <note>In wave theory, a ray is a line showing the direction in which the wave energy is traveling.</note> falling perpendicularly upon the

By placing the eyes in the plane that cuts the **crystal** <note>Iceland spar or calcite</note> along the short diameter of the top **rhombus** <term>rhombus: the diamond-shaped face of the natural crystal</term>, **regular refraction** <term>regular refraction: also called "ordinary" refraction, where light follows standard laws like in glass</term> will raise the letters to *B*, and one will see the bottom, and the letters on which it is placed, raised to *D* by **irregular refraction** <term>irregular refraction: also called "extraordinary" refraction, a phenomenon where light splits and moves at different speeds depending on its direction</term>. But I saw that if the plane N N were almost perpendicular to the plane G G, making the angle N C G—which is on side A—of 90 degrees and 40 minutes, the semi-spheroids N G N became similar to the semi-spheroids G N G, since the planes N N and G G were equally inclined, by an angle of 45 degrees and 20 minutes, upon the <term>axis: the central line of symmetry within the crystal's structure</term> s s. Consequently, if our theory were true, it was necessary that the surfaces produced by the section through N N should all produce the same refractions as the surfaces of the section through G G. And not only the surfaces of the section N N, but all the others produced by planes that were inclined to the axis s s by a similar angle of 45 degrees and 20 minutes. So that there was an infinity of cuts which ought to produce precisely the same refractions as the natural surfaces of the **crystal**, or as the cut parallel to any one of these surfaces, which is made by cleaving it <note>original: "fendant"; refers to the way calcite splits along natural flat planes</note>.

# ON LIGHT. CHAPTER V.

<meta>catchword: But</meta>

...the axis s s, as in the plane of this figure, the perpendicular ray should suffer no refraction; and that for oblique rays, there were different measurements for the irregular refraction, according to the situation of the plane in which the incident ray lay.

Now these things were indeed found to be so, and after that I could not doubt that a similar success would be encountered everywhere.

From which I concluded that one can form from this crystal solids similar to those that are natural to it, which will produce, on all their surfaces, the same regular and irregular refractions as the natural surfaces, and which nonetheless will cleave <note>original: "se fendront"; this refers to the way certain crystals split along flat, structural planes</note> quite differently, and not at all parallel to any of the faces.

That one can also make pyramids from it, having a square, pentagonal, or hexagonal base, or of as many sides as one wishes, where all the surfaces have the same refractions as the natural surfaces of the crystal, except for the base, which will not refract <note>original: "rompra"; literally 'break', the common 17th-century term for the bending of light</note> the perpendicular ray. Each of these surfaces will make an angle of 45 degrees 20 minutes with the axis of the crystal, and the base will be the section perpendicular to the axis.

Finally, that one can also make triangular prisms from it, or with as many sides as desired, where neither the sides nor the bases will refract the perpendicular ray, even though they all cause <term>double refraction: the phenomenon where a single ray of light enters a material and is split into two separate rays</term> for oblique rays. The cube is included among these prisms; its bases are sections perpendicular to the axis of the crystal, and its sides are sections parallel to this same axis.

From all this it further appears that the cause of its irregular refraction does not reside at all in the arrangement of the layers <note>original: "couches"; Huygens is arguing that the optical properties come from the microscopic shape of the particles, not just the visible layers of the stone</note> of which this crystal appears to be composed—and along which it cleaves in three different directions—and that it would be in vain to seek it there.

<meta>This page continues Christiaan Huygens' "Treatise on Light." Having mathematically described the laws of double refraction in Iceland Spar (calcite), he now provides practical advice for other experimenters on how to prepare the crystal's surfaces for observation. He concludes the page by introducing a new, unexplained phenomenon—what we now recognize as the polarization of light.</meta>

# T R E A T I S E

But so that anyone who possesses some of this stone might find, by their own experience, the truth of what I have just put forward, I will describe here the manner in which I went about cutting and polishing it. Cutting is easy using the cutting wheels of lapidaries <note>craftspeople who cut, shape, and polish gemstones</note>, or in the same way that one saws marble; but polishing is very difficult, and by employing the ordinary means, one dulls the surfaces much sooner than making them shine. 

After several attempts, I finally found that one must not use a metal plate for this purpose, but rather a piece of mirror glass made matte and unpolished. Upon this, with fine sand <note>original: "sablon"; a very fine-grained abrasive sand</note> and water, one gradually smooths this crystal, just as one does with eyeglass lenses, and one polishes it simply by continuing the work and constantly reducing the amount of abrasive material. I have not, however, been able to give it a perfect clarity and transparency; but the evenness that the surfaces acquire allows one to better observe the effects of refraction than in those surfaces made by cleaving the stone, which always have some inequality.

Even when the surface is only moderately smoothed, if one rubs it with a little oil or egg white <note>original: "blanc d'oeuf"; historically used as a temporary varnish to fill microscopic scratches</note>, it becomes very transparent, so that the refraction is revealed quite distinctly. And this aid is especially necessary when one wishes to polish the natural surfaces to remove their inequalities, because one cannot make them as shiny as those of the other sections, which take a polish all the better the less they resemble these natural planes.

Before finishing the treatise on this Crystal, I will add yet another marvelous phenomenon, which I discovered after having written all of the above. For although I have not been able to find its cause until now, I do not wish to omit pointing it out, in order to give others the opportunity to seek it. It seems that it would be necessary to make still other suppositions beyond those which

<meta>This page continues Christiaan Huygens' "Treatise on Light." In the preceding text, he discussed the method for polishing Iceland Spar (calcite) to observe its unique optical properties. ### OF LIGHT. CHAPTER V.

...that I have made; which will not, because of that, cease to maintain all their probability, after having been confirmed by so many proofs.

The phenomenon is, that by taking two pieces of this crystal <note>Iceland Spar or calcite</note> and placing one upon the other, or indeed holding them with some space between the two; if all the sides of one are parallel to those of the other, then a ray of light, such as **A B**, having been split in two within the first piece—namely into **B D** and **B C**, following the two refractions, regular and irregular—

<image-desc>Two side-by-side geometric diagrams illustrating the double refraction of light through two stacked crystals. Each diagram shows two parallelograms representing sections of crystal with a space between them. A single ray of light (A) enters the top crystal at point (B) and splits into two rays inside. In the left diagram, these two rays exit the first crystal at (C) and (D), pass through the gap to enter the second crystal at (E) and (G), and finally exit at (F) and (H). The right diagram shows a similar configuration with slightly different ray paths, demonstrating the consistency of the phenomenon when the crystal axes are parallel.</image-desc>

—upon penetrating from there into the other piece, each ray will pass through it without being split in two any further; but that which was produced by the regular refraction <term>regular refraction: the standard bending of light as it passes through a medium, now called "ordinary" refraction</term>, like **D G** here, will only undergo another regular refraction in **G H**, and the other, **C E**, an irregular <term>irregular refraction: also called "extraordinary" refraction, where the light bends at an unexpected angle due to the crystal's internal structure</term> one in **E F**. And the same thing happens not only in this arrangement, but also in all those where the principal section <note>the internal plane of the crystal that determines how the light is split</note>...

<meta>catchword: principal</meta>

<summary>Huygens describes a breakthrough experiment where light, after being split by one crystal, fails to split again when passing through a second, parallel crystal. This marks the first recorded observation of the polarization of light, though Huygens could not yet fully explain why it occurred.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Christiaan Huygens' "Treatise on Light," specifically Chapter V. This page follows the observation that when crystals are aligned, light split by the first crystal is not split further by the second.</meta>

### TREATISE

[princi]pal <note>original: "pale"; the end of the word "principale," meaning the principal section of the crystal</note>, of the one and of the other piece, is located in the same plane, without it being necessary for the two surfaces facing each other to be parallel. <summary>Huygens describes a "marvelous" phenomenon where light already split by one crystal refuses to split again in a second aligned crystal, but reverses its behavior when the second crystal is rotated 90 degrees. This experiment identifies the property of light we now call polarization, though Huygens describes it as the light "losing" the ability to move certain types of matter.</summary>

### ON LIGHT. CHAPTER V.

<margin>M 2</margin>
the lower piece; and on the contrary, the ray that came from the irregular refraction <term>irregular refraction: now known as "extraordinary refraction," where light does not follow the standard laws of refraction (Snell's Law)</term>, such as **C E**, now undergoes only a regular refraction.

But in all the other infinite positions, besides those that I have just determined, the rays **D G** and **C E** are each divided once again into two by the refraction of the lower crystal; so that from the single ray **A B**, four are produced, sometimes of equal clarity, sometimes some much dimmer than others, depending on the varying alignment of the positions of the crystals. However, these four rays together do not appear to have more light than the single ray **A B**.

When one considers here that—while rays **C E** and **D G** remain the same—it depends on the position given to the lower piece whether to divide each of them in two, or not to divide them at all, whereas the ray **A B** is always divided; it seems that one is obliged to conclude that the light waves, having passed through the first crystal, acquire a certain form or disposition <note>Huygens is describing the phenomenon of polarization—where light waves are oriented in a specific direction—though he did not yet have the technical term for it</note>, by which, upon meeting the texture <note>original: "tissu," referring to the internal molecular or structural arrangement</note> of the second crystal in a certain position, they are able to move the two different types of matter that serve the two species of refraction; and by meeting this second crystal in another position, they can only move one of these matters. But as for saying how this is done, I have found nothing so far that satisfies me.

Leaving this research to others, then, I pass to what I have to say regarding the cause of the extraordinary shape of this crystal, and why it cleaves easily in three different directions, parallel to any of its surfaces.

There are several vegetable bodies, minerals, and crystallized salts <note>original: "sels congelez," literally "frozen salts," referring to substances formed through precipitation or cooling</note> that form with certain angles and regular shapes. Thus, among flowers, there are many that have their petals <note>original: "feuilles," which can mean leaves or petals</note> arranged in regular polygons, numbering 3, 4, 5, or 6 sides, but no more. **Rock Crystal** <note>Huygens refers to quartz</note> usually grows in hexagonal rods, and one finds **diamonds** that are born with a square tip <note>likely referring to the octahedral or cubic crystal forms of diamond</note>, and polished surfaces. There is a species of small flat stones, stacked directly on top of each other, which are all pentagonal in shape, with rounded angles and the sides a little bent inward. The grains of **gray salt**, which are born from sea water, affect the figure, or at least the angle, of the **cube**; and in the freezing <note>crystallization</note> of other salts, and that of sugar, one finds other solid angles, with perfectly flat surfaces. Fine **snow** almost always falls formed in small stars with 6 points, and sometimes in hexagons whose sides are straight. And I have often observed, within water that is beginning to freeze, a sort of flat and slender leaves of **ice**, whose middle stripe throws out branches inclined at an angle of 60 degrees. All these things deserve to be researched carefully, to recognize how and by what artifice nature operates there. But it is not now my design to treat this matter entirely. It seems that in general the regularity, which is found in these productions, comes from the arrangement of the small invisible and equal particles <note>original: "particules invisibles," an early conceptualization of molecules or atoms</note> of which they are composed.

And to come to our **Iceland Crystal**, I say that if there were a pyramid like **A B C D**, composed of small round corpuscles, not spherical, but flat **spheroids** <term>spheroids: a sphere that has been flattened (oblate) or elongated (prolate); Huygens argues here for flattened, pancake-like particles</term>, such as would be made by the conversion of this

...ellipse GH on its minor diameter EF; of which the proportion to the major [diameter] is very nearly that of 1 to the square root of 8. I say then that the solid angle <note>the three-dimensional corner where three or more planes meet</note> at the point D would be equal to the obtuse <note>wide; greater than 90 degrees</note> and equilateral <note>having sides of equal length</note> angle of this crystal. I say further, that if these corpuscules <note>tiny, invisible building blocks of matter</note> were lightly glued together, in breaking this pyramid, it would break along faces parallel to those which form its point: and that by this means—as it is easy to see—it would produce prisms similar to those of the same crystal, such as represented in this other figure. The reason is that, in breaking in this way, an entire layer separates easily from its neighboring layer, because each spheroid <note>a sphere-like shape, here flattened like a lentil</note> detaches from only three spheroids of the other layer; of which three only one touches it by the flattened surface, and the other two only by the edges.

<image-desc>A detailed diagram illustrating a crystalline structure composed of multiple layers of rounded, lens-shaped, or ovoid elements. The structure forms a larger, somewhat pyramidal shape, with various points labeled A, B, C, D, G, H, K, L, M, suggesting a geometric analysis of its formation and cleavage planes. The layers are shown as tightly packed, forming a stable structure.</image-desc>

And what causes the surfaces to separate clean and polished is that if any spheroid of the neighboring layer were to come out of it to attach itself to the one being separated, it would have to detach itself from six other spheroids that hold it tight, and four of which press against it by these flattened surfaces.

Since then both the angles of our crystal and the manner in which it cleaves <term>cleaves: the natural tendency of a mineral to break along specific flat planes determined by its atomic structure</term> agree exactly with what is observed in a composite of such spheroids, it is a great reason to believe that its particles are formed and arranged in the same way.

There is even enough appearance that the prisms of this crystal are made by the breaking of pyramids, since Mr. Bartholin <note>Erasmus Bartholin (1625–1698), the Danish scientist who first discovered the double refraction of light in Iceland Crystal</note>

...that each of the <term>spheroids<note>Huygens’ term for the microscopic particles making up the crystal, which he envisioned as flattened spheres, much like lentils.</note></term> of a layer is hardly held at all by the six of the same layer that surround it, because they only touch at the edges; so that it easily adheres to the neighboring layer, and others to it, for the same reason; which causes uneven surfaces. As for the other division following the plane G H K L, one will see that each spheroid would have to detach from four of the neighboring layer, two of which touch it by the flattened surfaces, and two by the edges. Consequently, this division is also more difficult than that which occurs parallel to one of the surfaces of the crystal; where we said that each spheroid only detaches from three of its neighboring layer; of which only one touches it by the flattened surface, and the other two by the edges only.

However, what made me realize that there are layers of this latter sort in the crystal is that in a piece of half a pound which I have, one sees that it is split all the way along, like the aforementioned prism through the plane G H K L; which is apparent from the <term>rainbow colors<note>original: "couleurs d'Iris." All this proves, then, that the composition of the crystal is as we have said.

To which I add this further experiment: if one passes a knife by scraping over any of these natural surfaces, and does so descending from the obtuse equilateral angle—that is to say, from the point of the pyramid—it is found to be very hard; but by scraping in the opposite direction, it is easily notched. This clearly follows from the arrangement of the small spheroids; upon which,

I will not undertake to say anything concerning the manner in which so many small <term>corpuscles<note>Huygens uses this term to refer to the fundamental "building blocks" of matter.</note></term> are engendered, all equal and similar, nor how they are set in such a fine order. Whether they are formed first and then assembled; or whether they arrange themselves this way as they are born, and in proportion as they are produced, which seems more probable to me. To reveal truths so hidden would require a knowledge of nature much greater than that which we possess. I will only add that these small spheroids might well contribute to forming the spheroids of the waves of light, supposed above; both being situated in the same way, and with their axes parallel.

->*Lemma which has been assumed.*<-

If a <term>spheroid<note>A sphere-like surface that is slightly flattened or elongated; in this context, it represents the shape of the light wave traveling through the crystal.</note></term> is touched by a straight line, and also by two or more planes which are parallel to that line, although not necessarily parallel to each other; all the points of contact, both of the line and of the planes, will lie in the same ellipse, created by a plane passing through the center of the spheroid.

Let the spheroid L E D be touched by the line B M at point B, and also by planes, parallel to this line, at points o and A. It must be demonstrated that the points B, O, and A lie in the same ellipse, created within the spheroid by a plane passing through its center.

<image-desc>A geometric diagram of a spheroid with an external line B M touching it at point B, and several internal intersecting planes forming ellipses. Points L, E, D, B, M, P, H, Q, S, K, N, R, O, A are labeled within and around the spheroid, illustrating geometric relationships and sections.</image-desc>

Through the line B M and through the points o and A, let there be drawn planes parallel to one another, which, by cutting the spheroid, form the ellipses L B D, P O P, and Q A Q. These will all be similar, and similarly situated, and will have their centers K, N, and R located on the same diameter of the spheroid. This diameter will also be the diameter of the ellipse formed by the section of the plane that passes through the center of the spheroid and cuts the planes of the three aforementioned ellipses at right angles. For all of this is made manifest by Proposition 15 of Archimedes’ book on <term>Conoids and Spheroids<note>original: "Conoides & Spheroïdes d'Archimede." A foundational work by the Greek mathematician Archimedes concerning the properties of solids formed by the revolution of conic sections.</note></term>. Furthermore, the two last

ON LIGHT. CHAP. V.

<sig>N 3</sig>

<meta>catchword: posed</meta>

last planes, which were drawn through points O and A, will also form straight lines by cutting the planes that touched the <term>spheroid<note>A sphere-like surface used by Huygens to model how light waves spread out in different directions at different speeds.</note></term> at these same points, such as OH and AS. These will be, as is easy to see, parallel to BM; and all three—BM, OH, and AS—will touch the ellipses LBD, POP, and QAQ at these points B, O, and A, since they lie within the planes of these ellipses and, at the same time, in the planes that touch the spheroid.

If now from these points B, O, and A, one draws straight lines BK, ON, and AR through the centers of these same ellipses, and through these centers one also draws the diameters LD, PP, and QQ parallel to the <term>tangents<note>Lines that touch a curve at a single point without crossing it; here, they represent the edge of the light wave.</note></term> BM, OH, and AS, these diameters will be the <term>conjugates<note>In the geometry of ellipses, conjugate diameters are pairs of lines where each is parallel to the tangents at the endpoints of the other.</note></term> of the aforementioned BK, ON, and AR.

And because the three ellipses are similar and similarly positioned, and because they have their diameters LD, PP, and QQ parallel, it is certain that their conjugate diameters BK, ON, and AR will also be parallel. Since the centers K, N, and R are, as has been stated, on the same diameter of the spheroid, these parallels BK, ON, and AR will necessarily lie in the same plane, which passes through this diameter of the spheroid. Consequently, the points B, O, and A lie in the same ellipse formed by the intersection of this plane. Which was to be proven. And it is manifest that the demonstration would be the same if, besides points O and A, there were others in which the spheroid was touched by planes parallel to the line BM.

...posed regarding the nature of light, and of opaque and <term>transparent bodies<note>The original term is "diaphanes," referring to materials like glass or water that allow light to pass through.</note></term>; I shall here show a very easy and natural way to deduce, from the same principles, the true shapes which serve, either by reflection or by refraction, to gather or to disperse the rays of light as one desires. For although I do not see that there is a way to make use of these shapes in the matter of Refraction—both because of the difficulty of shaping <term>telescope glasses<note>Specifically lenses for refracting telescopes.</note></term> with the required accuracy, and because there is in refraction itself a property that prevents the perfect meeting of rays, as <term>Mr. Newton</term> has very well proven through experiments; I shall nonetheless report the discovery of them, since it offers itself, so to speak, on its own, and because it further confirms our Theory of refraction by the agreement found here between the <term>refracted ray<note>Original: "rayon rompu," literally "broken ray."</note></term> and the reflected one. Besides, it may be that some utility will be discovered in the future that is not seen at present.

To come then to these shapes, let us first suppose that one wishes to find a surface <term>C D E</term> which gathers the rays coming from a point <term>A</term> to another point <term>B</term>: and that the summit of the surface is the point <term>D</term>, given on the straight line <term>A B</term>. I say that, whether by reflection or by refraction, one need only make this surface such that the path of the light, from point <term>A</term> up to all points of the curved line <term>C D E</term>, and from these to the point of meeting—as is here the path through the straight lines <term>A C, C B</term>, through <term>A L, L B</term>, and through <term>A D, D B</term>—is made everywhere in equal times: by which the discovery of these curves becomes very easy.

For as far as the reflecting surface is concerned, since the sum of the lines <term>A C, C B</term> must be equal to that of <term>A D, D B</term>, it appears that <term>D C E</term> must be an ellipse; and for refraction, having assumed the proportion of the speeds of light waves in the <term>transparent bodies</term> <term>A</term> and <term>B</term> to be known—for example, from 3 to 2 (which is the

Because the sums of the travel times along A K, K B, and along A C, C B are equal; if from the first sum one subtracts the time along K B, and from the other the time along R B; there will remain the time along A K equal to the time along these two, A C, C R. <note>Huygens is proving that light reaching the boundary at different points arrives at the new wavefront simultaneously.</note> Consequently, in the time that the light has traveled along A K, it will also have traveled along A C, and furthermore, a particular <term>spherical wave</term> <note>Huygens’ Principle: every point on a primary wavefront acts as a source of secondary spherical "wavelets."</note> will have formed within the <term>transparent medium</term> <note>Original: "diaphane." And so on for all the other points of the curve C D E. Therefore, at the moment the light has arrived at K, the arc K R S will terminate the movement that spread from A over D C K. And thus this same arc will be, within the <term>transparent medium</term>, the <term>propagation</term> <note>The process by which the wave spreads through the medium.</note> of the wave emanating from point A; which wave one can represent by the arc D N, or by some other closer to center A. But all the parts of the arc K R S are then extended along straight lines which are perpendicular to it, that is to say, which tend toward center B (for this is demonstrated in the same way that we proved above that the parts of spherical waves extend along straight lines coming from their center) and these progressions of the parts of the waves form the <term>rays</term> of light themselves. It appears, therefore, that all these rays tend here toward point B.

As for the other part, Descartes remarked that it would serve for refractions if there were some mirror material of such a nature that through it the <term>force of the rays</term> <note>Descartes used the term "force" because he believed light moved instantaneously. But we have shown that, in our manner of explaining <term>reflection</term>, this cannot come from the material of the mirror, and that it is entirely impossible.

From what has been demonstrated regarding this oval, it will be easy to find the figure that serves to assemble parallel <term>incident rays</term> <note>Incoming light rays striking a surface.</note> toward a point. For by supposing the exact same construction, but with point $A$ infinitely distant—which produces parallel rays—our oval becomes a true **Ellipse**; the construction of which differs in nothing from that of the oval, except that $F C$ is here a straight line, perpendicular to $D B$, which previously was an arc of a circle. For the <term>wave of light</term> $D N$, being likewise represented by a straight line, it will be shown that all points of this wave, extending to the surface $K D$ by lines parallel to $D B$, will then advance toward point $B$, and will arrive there at the same time. As for the manner in which Mr. Descartes found these lines, since he did not explain it, nor has anyone else since then to my knowledge, I will state here, in passing, how it seems to me it must have occurred. Suppose we seek to find the surface made by the <term>revolution</term> <note>The physical rotation of a 2D curve around an axis to create a 3D volume, in this case, a lens shape.</note> of the curve **KDE**, which, receiving the <term>incident rays</term> <note>Light rays originating from a source and traveling toward a surface.</note> that come to it from point **A**, diverts them toward point **B**. Considering then this curve as already known, and let its vertex be **D**...

Let us now return to our method, and see how it leads without difficulty to finding the lines required for one side of the **glass** <note>In this context, Huygens uses "glass" to refer to the lens itself.</note>, when the other is of a given figure; not only plane or spherical, or made by one of the **Conic sections** <term>Conic sections: curves such as the ellipse, parabola, or hyperbola formed by the intersection of a plane and a cone.</term> (which is the restriction with which **Descartes** proposed this problem, leaving the solution to those who would come after him) but generally any figure whatsoever: that is to say, one made by the revolution of any given curved line, to which one only knows how to draw straight tangent lines.

Let the given figure be made by the conversion of some such curve <term>AK</term> around the axis <term>AV</term>, and let this side of the glass receive rays coming from point <term>L</term>. Let, furthermore, the thickness <term>AB</term> of the middle of the glass be given, and the point <term>F</term> at which one wants all the rays to be perfectly reunited; whatever the first refraction, made at the surface <term>AK</term>, might have been.

I say that for this, it is only necessary that the line <term>BDK</term>, which forms the other surface, be such that the path of the light, from point <term>L</term> to the surface <term>AK</term>, and from there to the surface <term>BDK</term>, and from there to point <term>F</term>, is made everywhere in equal times, and each equal to the time that the light employs to pass through the straight line <term>LF</term>, of which the part <term>AB</term> is in the glass.

Let <term>LG</term> be a ray falling upon the arc <term>AK</term>. Its refraction <term>GV</term> will be given by means of the tangent that one will draw at point <term>G</term>. Now it is necessary to find on <term>GV</term> the point <term>D</term>, such that <term>FD</term> with $\frac{3}{2}$ of <term>DG</term> and the straight line <term>GL</term>, are equal to <term>FB</term> with $\frac{3}{2}$ of <term>BA</term> and the straight line <term>AL</term>; which, as it appears, make a given length. <note>The fraction 3/2 represents the refractive index of glass used by Huygens, implying light travels 1.5 times slower in glass than in air.</note> Or rather, by removing from both sides the length of <term>LG</term>, which is also given, one only needs to draw <term>FD</term> upon the straight line <term>VG</term>, such that <term>FD</term> with $\frac{3}{2}$ <term>DG</term> is equal to a given line; which is a very easy <term>plane problem</term> <note>A "plane problem" in classical geometry is one that can be solved using only a straightedge and a compass.</note>: and the point <term>D</term> will be one of those through which the curve <term>BDK</term> must pass. And likewise, having drawn another ray <term>LM</term>, and found its refraction <term>MO</term>, one will find—

One may demonstrate in the same way that at this same moment, the light coming by any other ray, such as **LM**, **MN**, will have spread a movement that is terminated by the arc **BP**. --- Page 132 ---
<meta>This page continues Huygens' investigation into "irregular refraction." Having established that light behaves as a wave, he now uses geometry to demonstrate how a wave front changes shape as it passes through a spherical surface, moving away from a simple circular arc into a more complex curve.</meta>

<page-num>120</page-num>
<header>T R E A T I S E</header>

According to what has been shown above, the straight line **AD**, which is drawn from the summit of the sphere perpendicular to its axis (to which the rays arrive parallel), represents the **wave of light**; and in the time that its end **D** has reached the spherical surface **AGE** at **E**, its other parts will have encountered the same surface at **F, G, H** etc., and will have further formed individual spherical waves, of which these points are the centers. And the surface **EK**, which all these waves will touch, will be the propagation of the wave **AD** within the sphere, at the moment that the end **D** has come to **E**. Now the line **EK** is not an arc of a circle, but it is a curved line made by the **Evolution** <term>evolution: a geometric method where a curve (the involute) is traced by the end of a taut thread as it is unwrapped from another curve (the evolute)</term> of another curve **ENC**, which touches all the rays **HL, GM, FO**, etc., which comprise the refractions of the parallel rays; by imagining that there is a thread laid upon the convexity **ENC**, which, by unwrapping, describes with its end **E** the aforementioned curve **EK**. For, assuming that this curve is thus described, we shall demonstrate that the said waves formed from the centers **F, G, H**, etc., will all touch it.

It is certain that the curve **EK**, and all the others described by the evolution of the curve **ENC** with different lengths of thread, will intersect all the rays **HL, GM, FO** etc. at right angles, and in such a way that their parts, intercepted between two such curves, will all be equal; for this follows from what has been demonstrated in our treatise *On the Motion of Pendulums* <note>original: *De Motu Pendulorum*. Huygens refers here to his 1673 work *Horologium Oscillatorium*, where he laid the mathematical foundation for the study of evolutes and involutes</note>. Now, imagining the incident rays as infinitely close to one another, if one considers two of them, like **RG** and **TF**, and one draws **GQ** perpendicular to **RG**, and the curve **FS**, which intersects **GM** at **P**, is described by the evolution of the curve **NC**, starting from **F**, as far as I suppose the thread extends; one can take its particle **FP** for a straight line perpendicular to the ray **GM**, and similarly the arc **GF** as a straight line. But **GM** being the refraction of the ray **RG**, and **FP** being perpendicular to it, it is necessary that **QF** be to **GP**

<meta>catchword: as</meta>

<summary>Huygens explains that a light wave passing through a sphere takes on a new, non-circular shape (an involute) determined by the "evolution" of a secondary curve that touches all refracted rays. He cites his own mathematical work on pendulums to prove that these evolving wave fronts always intersect light rays at right angles.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the geometric proof from page 120. Huygens is demonstrating that the wavefront inside a medium (the curve EK) is formed by the envelope of countless smaller spherical waves. He uses the refractive ratio of 3 to 2—a common value for the refractive index of glass in the 17th century—to show how the distance traveled by light changes as it enters a new medium.</meta>

<page-num>121</page-num>
<header>ON LIGHT. CHAPTER VI.</header>

...as 3 to 2, that is to say, in the proportion of refraction; as was shown above in explaining the invention of Descartes <note>original: "l'invention de Des Cartes"—referring to the Law of Refraction, or Snell's Law, which René Descartes published in 1637</note>. And the same thing happens in all the small arcs **GH**, **HA**, etc. Namely, that in the quadrilaterals <note>quadrilateral: a four-sided geometric figure</note> that enclose them, the side parallel to the axis is to its opposite as 3 to 2.

<image-desc>A geometric diagram illustrating optical wave propagation and refraction. A vertical line on the left contains points D, B, E. From these, various rays and arcs extend toward the right. A large arc curves from top to bottom. A secondary curve, labeled EK, intersects several straight rays (FO, FM). The diagram is densely populated with labels: T, R, Q, G, H, A, S, K, X, V, N, Y, and lowercase letters a, b, c, d, e, f, g, h, o, M, L, C toward the bottom. Therefore, also as 3 to 2, so shall be the sum of the one to the sum of the others; that is to say, **TF** to **AS**, and **DG** to **AK**, and **BE** to **SK** or **DV**, assuming that **V** is the intersection of the curve **EK** and the ray **FO**. But, making **FB** perpendicular to **DE**, as 3 is to 2, so also is **BE** to the semi-diameter <note>semi-diameter: the radius of a circle or sphere</note> of the spherical wave emanating from point **F**, during the time that the light outside the transparent medium <term>diaphane: a transparent substance or medium through which light can pass, such as glass or water</term> has passed through the space **BE**; therefore it appears that this wave will cut the ray **FM** at the same point **V**, where it is cut at right angles by the curve **EK**, and that consequently the wave will touch this curve. One will prove in the same manner that it is so for all the other aforementioned waves, originating from points **G**, **H**, etc., namely that they will touch the curve **EK**, in the mo-

<meta>This page continues the geometric explanation of wave propagation through a medium, specifically focusing on the formation of caustic curves—where light rays intersect and wavefronts "fold" or evolve. It follows the demonstration of how the wavefront EK is constructed based on the refractive ratio of 3 to 2.</meta>

<page-num>122</page-num>
<header>T R E A T I S E</header>

...ment <note>original: "tellement"—so; continuing from the previous page's "so that the light..."</note> that the position *D* of the wave *E D* will have reached *E*.

To explain now what becomes of these waves after the rays begin to cross: it is from that point that they fold back <note>fold back: Huygens is describing the formation of a "caustic," a curve to which all the light rays are tangent, causing the wavefront to double back on itself</note>, and are composed of two parts that hold together; one being a curve made by the evolution <note term="evolution">In geometry, the evolution (evolute) of a curve is the locus of all its centers of curvature. Huygens uses this to explain how the shape of a wavefront changes as it moves.</note> of the curve *E N C* in one direction, and the other by the evolution of the same curve in the other direction. Thus, the wave *K E*, in advancing toward the meeting point, becomes *a b c*, of which the part *a b* is formed by the evolution of *b c*, a portion of the curve *E N C*, while the end *c* remains attached; and the part *b c* by the evolution of the portion *b E*, while the end *E* remains attached. Subsequently, the same wave becomes *d e f*; then *g h k*; and finally *c γ*; from which it then extends without any further folding, but always along curved lines, which are formed by the evolution of the curve *E N C*, increased by some straight line on the side of *c*.

There is even, *in this curve here*, a part *E N* which is straight, *N* being the point where the perpendicular from the center of the sphere *x* falls upon the refraction of the ray *D E*, which I now suppose touches the sphere. And it is from the point *N* that the folding of the light waves begins, up to the extremity of the curve *c*; which is found by making *A C* to *C X* be in the refractive ratio, as here 3 to 2.

One also finds as many other points as one wishes of the curve *E N C* by a Theorem demonstrated by Mr. Barrow <note>Isaac Barrow (1630–1677) was an English mathematician and the first Lucasian Professor at Cambridge; he was a precursor to Newton in the development of calculus and optics.</note> in the 12th of his *Optical Lectures* <note>original: "Leçons Optiques"—published as *Lectiones Opticae* in 1669</note>, though for another purpose. And it is to be remarked that a straight line can be given equal to this curve. For since, together with the straight line *N E*, it is equal to the straight line *C K*, which is known (because *D E* to *A K* is in the refractive ratio), it appears that by taking *E N* from *C K*, the remainder will be equal to the curve *N C*.

One will find folded waves in the same way in reflection

<page-num>123</page-num>
# ->ON LIGHT. CHAP. VI.<-

...of a concave spherical mirror. Let ABC be the section through the axis of a hollow hemisphere, whose center is D and whose axis is DB, upon which I suppose the rays of light arrive parallel to one another.

<image-desc>A geometric diagram illustrating the reflection of parallel light rays within a concave spherical mirror. It depicts a semicircle (ABC) with center D. Parallel rays entering from the left reflect off the arc AB, converging to form a caustic curve (labeled AFE). Point E is the focal point, located at the midpoint of the radius BD. Geometrical constructions with letters A through P show the mathematical derivation of the reflected wavefronts and the caustic shape.</image-desc>

All the reflections of these rays which fall upon the quadrant AB will touch a curved line AFE, whose endpoint E is at the focus of the hemisphere—that is to say, at the point which divides the radius <note>original: "demidiametre"—literally the half-diameter.</note> BD into two equal parts. The points through which this curve must pass are found by taking some arc AO starting from A, and making the arc OP double it; one must then divide the chord <note term="chord">Huygens uses "soutendente" (subtend), the straight line connecting two points on a curve. In modern geometry, this is the chord of the arc.</note> of the arc AP at point F, in such a way that the part FP is triple the length of FO; for then F is one of the required points.

And as parallel rays are merely the perpendiculars of the waves falling upon the concave surface—which waves are parallel to AD—it will be found that as they come to meet the surface AB, they form, upon reflecting, "folded waves" <note>Huygens is describing the complex shape of a wavefront after it hits a curved mirror; it doesn't stay a simple arc but doubles back on itself.</note> composed of two curves that arise from two opposite evolutions <note term="evolution">Also known as an "evolute." In geometry, if you "unroll" a string from a curve, the end of the string describes a new curve called the involute. Huygens uses this to show how the shape of the light wave is physically "unrolled" from the caustic curve AFE.</note> of the parts of the curve AFE. Thus, taking AD for an incident wave, when the part AG has met the surface AI—that is to say, when the point G has reached I—it will be the curves HF and FI, born from the evolutions of the curves FA and FE (both starting from F), which together constitute the propagation of the part AG. A little later, when the part AK has met the surface AM, being...

<meta>This page concludes Christiaan Huygens's "Treatise on Light," specifically the sixth chapter regarding the reflection of light. <page-num>124</page-num>
# TREATISE ON LIGHT. CHAP. VI.

...point K to M, then the curves LN and NM together will constitute the propagation of that part of the wave. And thus this **folded wave** <note>Huygens uses this term to describe the wave front after it has been reflected and doubled back on itself.</note> will continue to advance until the point N has reached the focus E. The curve AFE can be seen in smoke, or in flying dust, when a concave mirror is held up to the sun; and it should be known that it is nothing other than the curve described by point E on the circumference of circle EB, when that circle is rolled upon another whose radius <note>original: "demidiametre"</note> is ED, and whose center is D. So that it is a type of **Cycloid** <note term="Epicycloid">While Huygens uses the general term 'cycloid,' this specific shape—formed by rolling a circle on another circle—is known today as an epicycloid. This specific one is often called a nephroid.</note>, but one whose points can be found geometrically.

<image-desc>A geometric diagram depicting the reflection of light waves and the resulting caustic curve within a circle. Arcs representing waves are labeled with letters H, I, L, M, P, and O. A sharp, heart-shaped curve labeled A F E represents the caustic formed by the reflected light. A smaller circle is shown centered at D, with its radius extending to point E. Additional geometric construction lines and points like F and N are included within the circular area.</image-desc>

Its length is exactly equal to 3/2 of the diameter of the sphere; this is found and demonstrated by means of these waves, in much the same way as the measurement of the previous curve—though it could also be demonstrated by other methods which I shall omit, as they are outside the subject. The area AOBEFA, bounded by the arc of the quadrant, the straight line BE, and the curve EFA, is equal to the fourth part of the quadrant DAB.

# PREFACE.

Nature acts through methods so secret and imperceptible, in bringing toward the Earth those bodies we call heavy <note>original: "pesants"</note>, that no matter how much attention or diligence is applied, the senses can discover nothing of them. This is what forced the Philosophers of past centuries to seek the cause of this remarkable effect only within the bodies themselves, and to attribute it to some internal and inherent quality which made them tend downward and toward the center of the Earth, or to a desire <note>original: "appetit"; this refers to the Aristotelian idea that matter has a "natural inclination" or soul-like drive to reach its proper place.</note> of the parts to unite with the whole. This was not explaining the causes, but assuming obscure and misunderstood Principles. One can forgive those who contented themselves with such solutions in many instances; but not so easily Democritus <note>Democritus (c. 460–370 BCE) was an ancient Greek philosopher who first proposed that the universe is made of tiny, indivisible "atoms."</note> and those of his Sect, who, having undertaken to explain everything through Atoms, excepted Gravity alone; which they attached 

<vocab>Nature, Gravity, Philosophers, Democritus, Atoms</vocab>

<summary>In this preface, Huygens introduces his investigation into gravity, criticizing earlier philosophers who explained weight as an "inherent quality" or a "desire" of matter rather than a mechanical process.</summary>
<keywords>gravity, weight, Democritus, atomism, Aristotelian physics, mechanical philosophy, history of science</keywords>

--- Page 140 ---
<meta>This page continues Huygens's critique of historical theories of gravity. Having dismissed ancient views on the previous page, he now examines "modern" mechanical and immaterial theories before introducing the approach of René Descartes.</meta>

<page-num>126</page-num>

->P R E F A C E<-

to terrestrial bodies, and even to the Atoms themselves, without inquiring from where it might come to them. Among the modern authors and restorers of Philosophy, several have correctly judged that it was necessary to establish something outside of the bodies to cause the attractions and the repulsions <note>original: "fuites", literally "flights" or "escapes," referring here to bodies moving away from one another.</note> observed in them; but they have gone hardly any further than those first [philosophers], when they have had recourse—some to a subtle and heavy air, which by pressing upon bodies made them descend; (for this is already to assume a gravity <term>gravity: used here in the sense of "heaviness" or weight</term>, and it is so strongly against the laws of Mechanics to want a liquid and heavy matter to press downward upon the bodies it surrounds, when on the contrary it ought to make them rise, assuming they have no weight in themselves, just as water makes an empty vial rise when one sinks it into it;) others to spirits and to immaterial emanations; which clarifies nothing, since we have no conception of how that which is immaterial gives motion to a physical substance.

Mr. Descartes <note>René Descartes (1596–1650), the French philosopher who argued that all natural phenomena should be explained through the shape, size, and motion of matter.</note> has better recognized than those who preceded him, that we would never understand anything further in Physics than what we could relate to Principles that do not exceed the reach of our minds—such as those which depend on bodies, considered without qualities, and their motions. But as the greatest difficul-

<meta>catchword: ty</meta>

<vocab>Atoms, Philosophy, Mechanics, gravity, emanations, Descartes, Physics, principles</vocab>

<summary>Huygens dismisses theories that explain gravity through "heavy air" or "immaterial spirits," arguing that they either assume what they mean to prove or defy mechanical logic. He praises Descartes for establishing that physics must be based on understandable principles like matter and motion.</summary>
<keywords>mechanical philosophy, air pressure, immaterial spirits, René Descartes, laws of mechanics, physical substance</keywords>

--- Page 141 ---
<meta>This page continues Christiaan Huygens's critique of René Descartes's mechanical philosophy. While the previous page acknowledged Descartes’s shift toward explaining nature through matter and motion, it ended by noting the difficulty in applying these principles to specific phenomena like gravity.</meta>

<page-num>127</page-num>

->P R E F A C E.<-

ty consists in showing how so many diverse things are brought about by these principles alone, it is in this that he has not very well succeeded in several particular subjects that he proposed to examine: among which is, in my opinion, that of Gravity <term>Gravity: In this period, the term "pesanteur" (heaviness) referred to the quality of falling toward the Earth, which Huygens sought to explain through mechanical motion rather than an inherent property of matter.</term>. One may judge this by the remarks I make in several places regarding what he wrote about it; to which I could have added others. And yet, I confess that his attempts and his views, although false, served to open the way for me toward what I have discovered on this same subject.

I do not present it as being exempt from all doubt, nor as something to which objections cannot be raised. It is too difficult to reach such a point in research of this nature. I believe, however, that if the primary hypothesis on which I base my work is not the true one, there is little hope that it can be found while remaining within the limits of true and sound Philosophy <note>Huygens is referring to "Mechanical Philosophy," a 17th-century movement that rejected "occult" or magical explanations in favor of physical causes like impact and pressure.</note>.

Furthermore, what I bring here, insofar as it concerns only the cause of Gravity, will not appear new to those who have read the *Treatise on Physics* by Mr. Rohault <note>Jacques Rohault (1618–1672) was a French physicist whose textbook became the standard manual for Cartesian science in the late 17th century.</note>; because my theory is reported there almost in its entirety. For this philosopher, having seen my **Experiment of the rotating water**, and having heard the application I made of it (as he...

recognizes with candor, <note>In this context, the French "ingénuité" refers to frankness or openness rather than cleverness.</note>) has found enough plausibility in my opinion to wish to follow it. However, because he somewhat mixes my thoughts with those of Mr. Descartes <note>René Descartes (1596–1650), the influential philosopher who sought to explain the universe through mechanical laws.</note> and his own, and because he omits several things pertaining to this matter—some of which he could not have known—I was very pleased to have people see how I have treated it myself.

The greater part of this Discourse was written during the time I lived in Paris, and it is held in the Registers of the Royal Academy of Sciences <term>Royal Academy of Sciences: Founded in 1666 by Louis XIV; Huygens was invited to Paris to be one of its founding members and its most prominent scientist.</term>, up to the point where it discusses the alteration of Pendulums <term>Pendulums: Huygens discovered that the period of a pendulum's swing changes depending on its latitude, which provided evidence for the Earth's rotation and its non-spherical shape.</term> by the motion of the Earth. The rest was added several years later; and subsequently, the "Addition" was also included, for the reasons that will be found indicated at its beginning.
