# DISCOURSE
### *Concerning the Cause*
# OF GRAVITY.

To find an intelligible cause <note>By "intelligible cause," Huygens means an explanation based on the laws of mechanics—logic and physical contact—rather than "occult" or magical properties.</note> of Gravity, we must see how it can be achieved while assuming nothing in nature except bodies made of the same matter, in which we consider no quality nor any inclination to approach one another, but only different sizes, shapes, and movements; how, I say, it can be that many of these bodies nevertheless tend directly toward the same center and remain gathered around it—which is the most common and principal phenomenon of what we call gravity.

The simplicity of the principles I admit does not leave much choice in this investigation. For one judges immediately that there is no appearance of attributing to the shape, nor to the smallness of the corpuscules <term>Corpuscules: The tiny, indivisible particles of matter that scientists of this era believed made up the physical world, similar to our modern concept of atoms or molecules.</term>, any effect similar to that of gravity; which, being an effort or an inclination toward movement, must likely be produced by a movement. So it only remains to seek in what manner it can act, and in which bodies it can be found.

<vocab>Gravity, cause, matter, corpuscules, movement, principles</vocab>

<summary>Huygens begins his discourse by establishing a mechanical framework for gravity, arguing that it must be caused by the motion of matter rather than any innate "attraction" or hidden quality of objects.</summary>
<keywords>gravity, mechanical philosophy, corpuscules, laws of motion, physics, Christiaan Huygens</keywords>

--- Page 146 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the "Discourse on the Cause of Gravity." While the previous page established that gravity must be explained through the motion of matter (specifically "corpuscules"), this page narrows the focus to circular motion and contrasts the author's theory with that of René Descartes.</meta>

<page-num>130</page-num>

-> # DISCOURSE ON THE CAUSE <-

When looking simply at bodies, without that quality we call gravity <note>original: *pesanteur*. In the 17th century, "gravity" was often viewed as an inherent "heaviness" or "heft" rather than a force acting at a distance.</note>, their motion is naturally either straight or circular. The former belongs to them when they move without hindrance: the other when they are held around some center, or when they turn upon their own center. We are somewhat acquainted with the nature of straight motion, and the laws that bodies observe in the communication of their movements when they collide. But as long as one considers only this sort of motion, and the reflections <note>In this context, "reflections" refers to how particles of matter bounce off one another according to the laws of impact.</note> that occur between the parts of matter, one finds nothing that determines them to tend toward a center. It is therefore necessary to come to the properties of circular motion, and to see if there is any among them that can serve us.

I know that Mr. Descartes <note>René Descartes (1596–1650), a foundational figure in modern philosophy and science. He proposed that the universe was filled with a "plenum" of matter swirling in giant vortices, which pushed objects toward centers.</note> also tried in his *Physics* to explain gravity by the movement of certain matter that turns around the Earth; and it is a great deal to have been the first to have had this thought. But it will be seen, by the remarks I shall make in the course of this discourse, in what way his manner is different from that which I am going to propose, and also in what way it has seemed to me defective.

He considered, as I have, the effort made by bodies that turn circularly to move away from the center; an experience which does not allow us to doubt it. For in turning a stone in a sling, one feels that it pulls on the hand, and that this is all the stronger the faster one turns; to the point even that the cord may come to break. I have previously shown this same property of circular motion by attaching heavy bodies onto a round table, pierced at the center, and which turned on a pivot; and I have found the determination of its force, and several theorems concerning it: which can be seen at the end of the book I wrote on the *Movement of Pendulums* <note>original: *Mouvement des Pendules*. This refers to Huygens's 1673 masterpiece, *Horologium Oscillatorium*, where he mathematically defined centrifugal force.</note>. For example, I say that a body turning in a circle, at the end of a

a string extended horizontally, if it <note>Referring to a body or weight attached to the string.</note> moves with the speed it would acquire through its fall, by falling from a height equal to half of that same string—that is to say, a quarter of the diameter of the circle it describes—the string will be pulled with exactly as much force as if it were supporting the same body suspended in the air.

The effort to move away from the center <note>This is what we now call "centrifugal force." Huygens was one of the first to mathematically define it.</note> is therefore a constant effect of circular motion. And although this effect seems directly opposed to that of gravity, and although it has been objected to Copernicus <note>Nicolaus Copernicus (1473–1543), who proposed that the Earth rotates on its axis and orbits the Sun. Critics argued that if the Earth spun so fast, everything not nailed down would fly off into space.</note> that, by the rotation of the Earth in 24 hours, houses and men should be thrown into the air; I shall nonetheless show that this very effort, made by bodies turning in a circle to move away from the center, is the cause that other bodies converge toward the same center.

One can see this effect through an experiment that I have made specifically for this purpose, which well deserves to be noted, because it shows to the eye a visual image of gravity <note>French: *pesanteur*. At the time, scientists like Huygens were trying to explain gravity as a mechanical process rather than an innate attraction.</note>. I took a cylindrical vessel, about 8 or 10 inches in diameter, and whose bottom was white and smooth. Its height was only half or a third of its width. Having filled it with water, I threw into it some crushed sealing wax <term>Spanish wax: *cire d’Espagne*, a resinous material used for sealing letters, which is slightly denser than water</term>, which, being slightly heavier than the water, goes to the bottom; and afterward I covered it with a glass plate, applied immediately onto the water, which I fastened all around with cement, so that nothing could escape. Being thus adjusted, I placed this vessel in the middle of the round table, of which I spoke shortly before; and making it turn, I saw immediately that the bits of sealing wax, which touched the bottom and followed the movement of the vessel better than the water did, went to place themselves all around the edges, for the reason that they had more force than the water to move away from the center. But having continued for a little while to turn the vessel with the table, whereby the water acquired more and more circular motion, I suddenly stopped the table; and then, in that instant, all the sealing wax fled to the center in a heap, which represented to me the effect of

And the reason for this was that the water, despite the vessel being at rest, continued its circular motion and consequently its effort to move away from the center; whereas the sealing wax had lost this motion, or nearly so, by touching the bottom of the vessel which had been stopped. I also noticed that this powder moved toward the center in spiral lines, because the water still carried it along somewhat. But if one places in this vessel some body in such a way that it cannot follow the motion of the water at all, but can only move toward the center, it will then be pushed there in a straight line. For example, if **L** is a small ball that can roll freely on the bottom between the threads **AA**, **BB**, and a third slightly higher one **KK**, stretched horizontally across the middle of the vessel; one will see that as soon as the motion of the vessel is stopped, this ball will go to the center **D**. And it should be noted that in this last experiment, one can make the body **L** the same weight <note>French: *pesanteur*. In this context, Huygens refers to specific gravity or density relative to the fluid.</note> as the water, and the result will be even more successful; so that, without any difference in the weight of the bodies within the vessel, the motion alone produces the effect here.

The experiment that Mr. Descartes proposes, in one of his printed letters, differs greatly from this one. For he fills the vessel **ABC** with small lead shot <term>lead shot: *dragée de plomb*, tiny lead pellets used for hunting</term>, mixed with some pieces of wood or other material lighter than lead; and by making everything turn together, he says that the pieces of wood will be driven toward the middle of the vessel. This I can well believe, provided however that one taps lightly on the edges of the vessel to facilitate the separation of these two materials. But what happens here is in no way suitable for representing the effect of gravity; since one would have to conclude from this experiment that the bodies containing the least matter are those that weigh the most, which is contrary to what is observed in true gravity. letter, of throwing small pieces of wood into swirling water, and he says they will go toward the middle of the water. In which case, if he means wood that floats upon the water, as is likely, no concentration will occur. But if he intends for it to go to the bottom, it will truly be the same experiment that I proposed shortly before, and the wood will gather at the center; but this will be because, in touching the bottom of the vessel, its circular motion will be slowed <note>Huygens argues that friction against the container is the actual cause of the particles moving inward, a physical detail he believes Descartes overlooked.</note>, a reason of which Mr. Descartes made no mention.

Now, having found in nature an effect similar to that of gravity, and whose cause is known, it remains to be seen whether one can suppose that something similar happens with regard to the Earth; that is to say, whether there is some motion of matter that constrains bodies to tend toward the center, and which accommodates itself at the same time to all the other phenomena of gravity.

Supposing the daily motion of the Earth, and that the air and the **ether** <term>ether: *ether*, a hypothetical invisible substance then believed to fill all space and transmit physical forces.</term> surrounding it have this same motion, there is still nothing in that which should produce gravity: since, according to the experiment reported just before, terrestrial bodies should not follow this circular motion of **celestial matter**, but be as if at rest in relation to it, if they were to be pushed by it toward the center.

However, if one wanted the **celestial matter** to turn in the same direction as the Earth, but with much greater speed, it would follow that this rapid motion, of a matter that moves continually and all in the same direction, would be felt, and that it would carry away with it the bodies that are on the Earth; just as the water carries away the **sealing wax** <note>original: *cire d’Espagne*</note> in our experiment; which, however, does not happen at all. But besides that, this circular motion, around the axis of the Earth, could not in any case drive the bodies, which do not follow the sam-

<meta>This page continues Christiaan Huygens' critique of René Descartes' theory of gravity. On the previous page, Huygens argued that Descartes' explanation for why objects fall failed to account for the physical reality of friction and the specifics of how particles move in a container. Here, he begins to outline his own hypothesis: that gravity is caused by the rapid, multidirectional motion of a "fluid matter" surrounding the Earth.</meta>

<page-num>135</page-num>
<header>OF GRAVITY.</header>

...same motion, [acting] toward this same axis; so that we would not see heavy bodies falling perpendicular to the horizon, but along lines perpendicular to the **axis of the world** <note>The imaginary line through the North and South Poles around which the Earth rotates. Huygens is arguing that if gravity were simply caused by the Earth's rotation, objects would fall toward this line rather than straight down toward the Earth's center.</note>, which is again contrary to experience.

To explain gravity in the manner that I conceive of it, I shall suppose that within the spherical space which encompasses the Earth and the bodies around it to a great extent, there exists a **fluid matter** <term>fluid matter: *matiere fluide*, an invisible substance, similar to the "ether," composed of tiny particles moving at high speeds.</term> consisting of very small parts, which is variously agitated in all directions with great rapidity. Since this matter cannot leave this space, being surrounded by other bodies, I say that its motion must become partly circular around the center; not so much, however, that it all comes to turn in the same direction, but such that most of its different movements take place within spherical surfaces around the center of the said space, which for this reason also becomes the center of the Earth.

The reason for this circular motion is that matter contained within some space moves more easily in this way than by straight movements contrary to one another, which—even when reflecting back (since the matter cannot leave the space enclosing it)—are reduced to changing into circular ones.

One sees this effect of motion when testing silver by the **cupel** <term>cupel: *coupelle*, a small, shallow, porous vessel used by goldsmiths and chemists to separate precious metals from impurities like lead using high heat.</term>; for the small ball of lead mixed with silver, having its parts strongly agitated by heat, turns incessantly around its center, now to one side, now to another, changing at every moment, and so quickly that the eye has difficulty perceiving it. The same thing happens to a drop of **candle tallow** when, holding it suspended from the tip of a pair of **snuffers** <term>snuffers: *mouchettes*, a scissor-like tool used for trimming and extinguishing candle wicks.</term>, one brings it near the flame, for it begins to turn with a very great speed.

It is true that usually this drop turns entirely in one [direction]...

<meta>catchword: direction</meta>

<summary>Huygens rejects the idea that gravity pulls toward the Earth's axis and proposes instead that a rapidly vibrating fluid matter, constrained in a sphere, creates circular motions that drive objects toward the center of the Earth. He supports this with observations of molten metal in a cupel and spinning drops of candle tallow.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Christiaan Huygens' explanation of his theory of gravity. On the previous page, he proposed that the Earth is surrounded by a "fluid matter" whose particles move in circular paths within spherical layers. He now addresses why these particles don't just clash and stop, and why this fluid behaves differently than common liquids like water.</meta>

<page-num>136</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE ON THE CAUSE</header>

one side or another, according as the flame of the candle comes to touch it. But in the **celestial matter** <term>celestial matter: *matière céleste*, a theoretical invisible substance (often called ether) that Huygens believed filled the universe and transmitted forces like gravity and light.</term>, which I have supposed, the same must not happen; because having once [acquired] motion in all directions, it must always remain so, although it is changed into spherical [motion], because there is no reason why the motion of one part of the matter should prevail over that of the others, so as to make the whole mass turn in the same direction. For on the contrary, the **law of nature**, which I have reported elsewhere, is such in the collision of bodies which are diversely agitated, that there is always conserved therein the same quantity of motion <note>original: *quantité de mouvement*. This is an early concept of momentum (mass times velocity). Huygens is citing the principle of conservation of momentum, which he helped refine.</note> toward the same side.

And although these circular motions, in so many different directions within the same space, seem as if they ought to oppose and impede each other often, nonetheless the great mobility of the matter, aided by the smallness of its parts—which far surpasses the imagination—makes it so that it quite easily sustains all these different agitations. One sees, when one has stirred water in a glass vial, of how many different motions its parts are capable; and one must imagine the liquidity of the celestial matter to be incomparably greater than that which we observe in water; which, being composed of heavy parts, piled one upon the other, becomes thereby sluggish in motion; whereas the celestial matter, moving freely in all directions, very easily takes different impressions through the various encounters of its parts, or by the slightest impulse from other bodies. And if it were not so, the air would not yield as easily as it does to the motion of our hands. So it must be considered that the circular motions of this fluid matter around the Earth are very often interrupted and changed into others, but that there always remain more of them than of those following other paths: which suffices for the present purpose.

<summary>Huygens argues that gravity is caused by an extremely fine, highly mobile "celestial matter" that maintains its rapid circular motion around the Earth according to the laws of conservation. He compares its fluidity to water and air, suggesting its particles are so small and fast that they can move in many directions at once without stopping.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Christiaan Huygens' mechanical explanation of gravity. Having established that the space around the Earth is filled with a rapidly moving "fluid matter" (ether) circulating in all directions, he now explains how this motion creates the downward force we perceive as weight.</meta>

<page-num>137</page-num>
<header>->ON GRAVITY.<-</header>

It is not difficult now to explain how gravity is produced by this motion. For if, within the **fluid matter** <term>fluid matter: *matière fluide*, the subtle, invisible substance Huygens believed filled the universe; today we associate these theories with the "aether."</term> that revolves in the space we have supposed, there are found particles much larger than those composing it, or bodies made of a cluster of small parts hooked together, and if these bodies do not follow the rapid motion of the said matter, they will necessarily be pushed toward the center of the motion. There, they will form the **Terrestrial globe** if there is enough of them for that, assuming the Earth did not yet exist. And the reason is the same as that which, in the experiment reported above <note>Huygens is referring to a famous experiment where he rotated a vessel of water containing bits of sealing wax. As the water spun, the wax was driven to the center, demonstrating his mechanical theory of centrifugal force.</note>, causes the **sealing wax** <note>original: *cire d’Espagne*</note> to gather at the center of the vessel. It is in this, then, that the gravity of bodies likely consists: which one can say is the effort made by the fluid matter—which turns circularly around the center of the Earth in all directions—to move away from that center, and to push into its place the bodies that do not follow this motion.

Now, the reason why heavy bodies, which we see descending in the air, do not follow the spherical motion of the fluid matter, is quite manifest; because as there is this motion toward all sides, the impulses that a body receives from it succeed each other so suddenly that less time passes than it would need to acquire a noticeable motion. But as this reason alone is not enough to prevent the smallest bodies the eye can perceive, such as the **motes of dust** that fly in the air, from being chased here and there by the rapidity of this motion, it must be known that these small bodies do not float in the liquid matter that causes gravity alone; but that besides this one, there are other matters, composed of coarser particles, which fill the greater part of the space around us, and even those of the

<sig>T 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: heavens ;</meta>

<summary>Huygens defines gravity as a centrifugal effect where rapidly swirling ether tries to move away from the Earth, thereby forcing slower, heavier objects toward the center. He uses the analogy of sealing wax in a spinning bowl of water to illustrate this mechanical pressure.</summary>
<keywords>gravity, centrifugal force, ether, terrestrial globe, sealing wax experiment, fluid matter, physics of motion</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Christiaan Huygens' mechanical explanation of gravity. He is explaining why certain particles and bodies do not simply get swept up in the rapid circulation of the "fluid matter" (the ether) but are instead pushed toward the center of the Earth, creating the effect of weight.</meta>

<page-num>138</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE ON THE CAUSE</header>

heavens; these particles, although differently agitated and reflected among one another, do not follow the sudden motion of the **liquid matter**; because, being contiguous or very close to one another, too great a quantity of them would have to move at once. It is known that around the Earth, there are first the particles of **air**, which we will shortly show to be coarser than those of the **fluid matter** we have supposed. I say further that there is a matter whose particles are finer than those of air, but coarser than those of this fluid matter: which is proven by our experiment made with the **Air Pump** <term>Air Pump: *Machine qui vuide l'air*, a scientific instrument developed in the 17th century to evacuate air from a glass receiver, allowing scientists to study the properties of a vacuum.</term>. There, one observes the effect of an invisible matter that has weight where there is no air; since it supports water suspended in a glass tube, the open end of which is plunged into other water: and since it causes water to flow from a curved siphon, just as in the air—provided that the water, in these experiments, has been purged of air; which is done by leaving it for several hours in the **vacuum** <note>Huygens is describing experiments that today we understand through atmospheric pressure, but he is arguing for the existence of different grades of "subtle matter" that can even permeate glass.</note>. It appears from this first, that the particles of this heavy and invisible body are smaller than those of the air, since they pass through the glass which excludes the air, and that they make their weight perceived there. It appears further that they must be coarser than the particles of the fluid matter that causes **gravity** <note>original: *peſanteur*. Huygens uses this term to describe the downward force or the quality of being heavy.</note>, so that the body they compose does not follow the motion of that matter; for by following it, it would not be heavy. There may be around us yet other sorts of matter of different degrees of **fineness** <term>fineness: *tenuité*, referring to the extreme smallness or thinness of the particles.</term>, though all coarser than the matter that causes gravity. All of these will therefore contribute to preventing small specks of dust from being carried away by the rapid motion of this matter, because they do not follow this motion themselves.

Moreover, one should not find these different degrees strange 

<meta>catchword: of</meta>

<summary>Huygens argues that space is filled with various grades of matter; while the finest "fluid matter" causes gravity, coarser but still invisible particles (like those remaining in a vacuum pump) are what allow objects to retain their weight and resist being swept away by the ether's motion.</summary>
<keywords>gravity, air pump, vacuum, fluid matter, subtle matter, particles, siphon, atmospheric pressure</keywords>

--- Page 155 ---
<meta>This page continues Christiaan Huygens' mechanical explanation of gravity. On the previous page, he was discussing experiments with the air pump to prove that a "subtle matter" (even finer than air) exists because it can permeate glass and still exert pressure. He concludes here that this matter is made of tiny particles.</meta>

<page-num>139</page-num>

# ->ON GRAVITY<-

...of small **corpuscles** <term>corpuscles: A 17th-century term for tiny, indivisible particles of matter, similar to our modern concept of atoms or molecules.</term>, nor their extreme smallness. For although we have some inclination to believe that bodies, barely visible, are already almost as small as they can be, reason tells us that the same proportion that exists from a mountain to a grain of sand, this grain may have to another small body, and this one again to another, and that as many times as one might wish.

The extreme smallness of the parts of our **fluid matter** <term>fluid matter: Also known as "subtle matter" or ether. Huygens imagined space was filled with this rapidly moving, invisible fluid that pushes heavier objects toward the centers of planets.</term> is also of an absolute necessity to account for a significant effect of **gravity** <note>original: "pesanteur"</note>; which is that heavy bodies, enclosed on all sides in a vessel of glass, of metal, or of whatever other matter it may be, are found to always weigh equally. So it is necessary that the matter we have said to be the cause of gravity passes very freely through all the bodies that one deems the most solid, and with the same ease as through the air.

This is further confirmed by the fact that, if there were not this freedom of passage, a glass bottle would weigh as much as a solid body of glass of the same size; and that all solid bodies of equal **volume** would weigh equally; since, according to our **Theory**, the weight of each body is regulated by the quantity of the fluid matter that must rise in its place. <note>Huygens is applying the principle of buoyancy to gravity. He suggests that objects fall because the "subtle matter" is trying to move away from the center of the Earth, and the solid object "sinks" into the space the fluid leaves behind.</note>

This matter therefore passes easily into the interstices of the particles of which bodies are composed, but not through the particles themselves; and what causes the various weights, for example, of stones, metals, etc., is that those among these bodies, which are heavier, contain more such particles—not in number, but in volume: for it is in their place alone that the fluid matter can rise. But because one might doubt whether these particles, being **impenetrable** to the said matter, are for that reason entirely solid: (for if they were not, or even...

He explains that an object's weight is determined by the total volume of its solid particles, which displace this fluid matter.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Christiaan Huygens' mechanical explanation of gravity. On the previous page, he argued that gravity is caused by a "subtle matter" moving through objects. <page-num>140</page-num>

# DISCOURSE ON THE CAUSE

...[spaces] being empty, they should produce the same effect, for the reason I have just stated) I shall demonstrate that they have this perfect **solidity** <term>solidity: Here, Huygens uses "solidness" to refer to the density of matter within a body, meaning how much actual material it contains compared to empty space.</term>; and that, consequently, the **gravity** <note>original: "pesanteur"</note> of bodies follows precisely the proportion of the **matter** that composes them.

To this end, I shall draw attention to what happens in the **collision** of two bodies, when they meet in a horizontal movement. It is certain that the resistance made by bodies to being moved horizontally—such as a marble or lead ball placed on a very smooth table—is not caused by their weight toward the Earth, since lateral movement does not tend to move them away from the Earth, and thus it is in no way contrary to the action of gravity, which pushes them downward.

There is, therefore, nothing but the quantity of matter bound together, which each body contains, that produces this resistance: so that if two bodies contain as much as one another, they will rebound equally, or both remain without movement, depending on whether they are hard or soft. But experience shows that every time two bodies rebound thus equally or stop one another, having come to meet with equal speeds, these bodies are of equal gravity: therefore, it follows that those which are composed of an equal quantity of matter are also of equal gravity. This is what was to be demonstrated.

**Monsieur Descartes** <note>René Descartes (1596–1650), the famous French philosopher. While Huygens built upon Descartes' mechanical worldview, he frequently used experiments to correct Descartes' mathematical errors in physics.</note> was of another opinion in this matter, as also in what concerns the free passage of the matter that causes gravity through the bodies upon which it acts. For as to this last point, he maintains that this matter is prevented, by the encounter with the Earth, from continuing its movements in a straight line, and that for this reason, it moves away from it as much as it can. In this, he seems not to have thought of that property of gravity which I pointed out shortly before

...before. For if the movement of this matter is hindered by the Earth, it would no longer freely penetrate the bodies of metals nor that of glass. From which it would follow that lead enclosed in a vial would lose its weight with respect to the vial itself, or that at least this weight would be diminished. Furthermore, in carrying a heavy body to the bottom of a well, or into some deep quarry or mine, it ought to lose much of its **gravity** <note>original: "pesanteur." In this era, gravity was often viewed as an external push rather than an internal property of matter.</note> there. But it has not been found, as far as I know, by experience <note>Huygens uses "expérience" to mean both "experience" and "scientific experiment."</note> that it loses any weight whatsoever.

As for the other point, **Mr. Descartes** <note>René Descartes (1596–1650), the famous philosopher who proposed that gravity was caused by a "vortex" of subtle matter swirling around the Earth.</note> claims that, although a mass of gold is twenty times heavier than a portion of water of the same size, gold nevertheless may contain only 4 or 5 times as much matter as water. He argues this first because one must deduct (he should rather have said add) a weight equal to both, on account of the air in which they are weighed; and then because water and other liquids have a certain lightness compared to hard bodies, insofar as the parts of the former are in a state of continual motion.

But one can answer the first of these two reasons by noting that the gravity of the air around us, being only about 1 to 800 compared to that of water, is not a significant weight that must be added equally to that of the water and the gold as found by the **balance** <note>A weighing scale.</note>. And as for the other reason, if it were sound, it would follow that the same portion of water, after being frozen, would weigh much more than when it was liquid; and likewise, metals in a solid mass would weigh more than when they are melted; which is contrary to experience. Besides, I do not see how he conceived that the motion of the parts of liquid bodies would give them lightness—that is to say, a force to move away from the center—since for that it would be necessary for this movement to be circular around the center of the Earth, or for it to be stronger toward the top than toward the bottom, which he never said, but indeed...

For my part, I have carefully investigated the degree of this speed, and I believe I can determine approximately how high it must reach. lead weighs as much downward as it would weigh upward if, remaining at the same distance from the center of the Earth, it rotated around it with the same speed as the **fluid matter** <term>matiere fluide: the invisible, subtle particles that Cartesian physics assumed filled the universe and drove planetary motion.</term>. But I find by my **Theory of Circular motion** <note>Huygens refers to his work *De Vi Centrifuga* (On Centrifugal Force), where he derived the formula for centrifugal acceleration.</note>, which agrees perfectly with experiment, that for a body rotating in a circle—if one wants its effort to move away from the center to exactly equal the effort of its simple gravity—it must complete each revolution in the same amount of time that a **pendulum**, with a length equal to the radius of that circle, takes to make two swings. It is therefore necessary to see how much time a pendulum, with the length of the Earth's radius, would take for these two swings. This is easy to determine by the known property of pendulums and by the length of the one that beats seconds, which is 3 feet 8 1/2 lines, **Paris measure** <note>original: "mesure de Paris." The speed, therefore, of the fluid matter at the surface of the Earth must be equal to that of a body that would go around the Earth in this time of 1 hour, 24 1/2 minutes. Which speed is, very nearly, 17 times greater than that of a point under the **Equator**, which makes the same revolution, with respect to the **fixed stars** <note>This refers to the sidereal day, the time it takes the Earth to rotate once relative to the stars rather than the sun.</note> (as it must be taken here), in 23 hours, 56 minutes. This is apparent from the proportion between that time and that of one hour 24 1/2 minutes, which is very nearly as 17 to 1.

   Moreover, there are several natural effects that seem to require an extremely agitated matter, which easily penetrates the pores of bodies. Such is the force of **gunpowder** <term>poudre à Canon: black powder used in artillery. Huygens is arguing that the energy of the explosion must come from the motion of external "subtle" particles rather than the powder itself</term>, <unclear>r</unclear> which, upon igniting, does not take its violent motion from itself, nor from the one who brings the match to it; and consequently it must come from some other matter that possesses this motion, and which is found everywhere; producing its effect whenever it finds a suitable disposition. Such is also, as I conceive it, the force of the **Spring** <term>Reſſort: the elastic property of materials to return to their shape when bent or compressed</term>, both of steel and other solid bodies, as well as that of the air. This is done with such violence that I have seen it burst musket barrels in which water had been enclosed.

pressure of this matter, and consequently its speed is continually increased. Whereas, if the matter had only a moderate motion, the ball, after having acquired as much, would no longer accelerate its fall; because otherwise it would be obliged to push this same matter, and succeed in its place with more speed than the matter itself would have by its own motion.

One can finally find here the reason for the Principle that **Galileo** <note>Galileo Galilei (1564–1642), whose experiments with falling bodies led to the law of uniform acceleration.</note> took to demonstrate the proportion of the acceleration of falling bodies; which is that their speed increases equally in equal times. For as bodies are pushed successively by the parts of the matter which strives to rise in their place, and which, as we have just seen, acts continually upon them with the same force—at least in the falls that come under our experience—it is a necessary consequence that the increase in speeds be proportional to that of time.

Thus, I have explained, by a **Hypothesis** <term>Hypothese: a mechanical explanation based on the motion of invisible "subtle matter" rather than Newton's "action at a distance" which Huygens found philosophically problematic</term> which contains nothing impossible, why terrestrial bodies tend toward the center; why the action of gravity cannot be hindered by the interposition of any body known to us; why the internal parts of each body all contribute to its weight; and why, finally, falling bodies continually increase their speed, and do so in proportion to time. These are the properties of gravity that had been observed until now.

One more property remains, which until now was believed to be no less certain: that heavy bodies are just as heavy in one place on the Earth as in another. This having been found otherwise, by observations made recently, it is worth examining from where this can proceed, and what the consequences are.

I recognized, as soon as this new phenomenon was communicated to us, that its cause could be related to the daily motion of the Earth: which, being greater in each country according to how much closer it is to the Equinoctial line <note>The Equator.</note>, must produce a proportionate effort to throw bodies away from the center; and thereby take away from them a certain part of their weight. And it is easy, from the things explained above, to know what fraction this must be in bodies located under the Equator. For having found, as has been seen, that if the Earth rotated 17 times faster than it does, the centrifugal force under the Equator would be equal to the entire gravity of a body; it must be that the motion of the Earth, as it is now, removes a part of the gravity which is to the total gravity as 1 is to the square of 17, that is to say 1/289; because the forces of bodies to move away from the center around which they rotate are to each other as the squares of their speeds, following my third theorem *On Centrifugal Force* <note>original: *de Vi Centrifuga*. This refers to Huygens' major work on circular motion and the forces exerted by rotating bodies.</note>. ...we shall also see what part of the absolute weight the centrifugal effort <note>Known today as centrifugal force; the outward force felt by an object moving in a circle.</note> is at **D** or **H**. That is to say, the ratio of **DK** to **DH** will be known, as being composed of that of 289 to 1, and of **EC** to **DO**. But the angle **HDK** is also known, being equal to that of the Latitude of Paris, namely 48 degrees 51 minutes. Thus, we will know the ratio of **DK** to **KH**, which is the ratio of the absolute gravity of bodies to that which they have in Paris, and which is also the ratio of the length of the pendulum on a stationary Earth to the length it must have under this Parallel <note>The line of latitude passing through Paris.</note>, according to what has already been said. And since the length of the seconds pendulum <note>A pendulum that takes exactly one second to swing in one direction.</note> is given in Paris, we will also know what length the seconds pendulum would have on a stationary Earth, and what their difference is, and by how much this difference is less than that 1/289th part that we had found under the Equator.

To make this calculation with ease, and without the calculation of triangles, it must be known—and we will prove it now—that as the square of the radius **EC** is to the square of **DO**, the sine of the complement <note>The sine of the complement is what modern mathematicians call the "cosine."</note> of the Latitude of Paris, so is 1/289 (the difference or shortening of the pendulum under the Equator) to the difference or shortening in Paris. This is found by this means to be 1/668 of the length of the pendulum on the stationary Earth, or at the Pole. And since the seconds pendulum in Paris is 3 feet 8 5/6 lines <note>A "line" (*ligne*) is a pre-metric unit of length, equal to 1/12 of an inch.</note>; it follows that the length of the pendulum on the stationary Earth, or at the Pole, would be 3 feet 9 1/4 lines. From which, subtracting 1/668, which makes 1 1/2 lines, we will have the length of the seconds pendulum under the Equator as 3 feet 7 3/4 lines. So that this pendulum would be shorter than that of Paris by 5/6 of a line, which is a little less than what was found at Cayenne by Mr. Richer <note>Jean Richer (1630–1696), an astronomer whose observations in French Guiana in 1672 provided the first experimental evidence that the Earth's gravity varies by latitude.</note>, namely one line and a quarter.

But one cannot entirely trust these first observations, for which no circumstances are seen marked. And even less, I believe, to those which are said to have...

# ON GRAVITY

...seeking the shortening of the Pendulum in Paris (and it is the same thing in any other place whatsoever), when one knows the amount of this shortening under the Equator: let *KF* be taken, in the same figure, equal to *KH*, and let *HG* be parallel to the axis *PQ*. It has been shown that *HD* is to *DK* as the **effort to move away from the center** <note>This is what we now call centrifugal force.</note>, at *D* or *H*, is to the **absolute weight** <note>The weight of an object as it would be if the Earth were stationary, without the counteracting effect of rotation.</note> on the immobile Earth. But as *EC* or *CD* is to *DO*—that is to say, as *GD* is to *HD*—so is the centrifugal effort at *E*, under the Equator, to that at *D*. Therefore, as *GD* is to *DK*, so will be the centrifugal effort at *E* to the absolute weight on the immobile Earth. And the line *GD* will be the shortening of the pendulum required under the Equator, according to what was said previously. 

But *FD* is the shortening at Paris; and *GD* is to *DF* as the square of *GD* is to the square of *DH*; because the smallness of the angle *DKH* means that *HF* can be considered as perpendicular to *GD*. The shortening under the Equator, compared to that which belongs to Paris, is therefore as the square of *GD* is to the square of *DH*; that is to say, as the square of *CD*, or of *EC*, is to the square of *DO*. Which was to be demonstrated.

It remains to consider the angle *HKD* in the same figure, which marks by how much the lead weight *KH*, being at rest, deviates from the perpendicular *KD*. I find that, under the Parallel of Paris, this angle is 5 minutes 54 seconds; and that it must be even slightly larger at the 45th degree of Latitude.

This **declination** <note>Here, Huygens refers to the deflection of a plumb line from a line pointing directly to the Earth's center.</note> is quite contrary to what has been supposed, from time immemorial, as a very certain truth: namely, that the cord holding a suspended lead weight tends directly toward the center of the Earth. And this angle, of a tenth of a degree, is considerable enough to suggest that it should have been noticed, either in **Astronomical observations** or in those made with a <term>Level</term> <note>An instrument used to determine a horizontal line. Huygens points out that if gravity doesn't pull straight to the center, "horizontal" is not a simple arc around that center.</note>. For to speak only of these latter...

--- Page 168 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the physical consequences of the Earth's rotation. The previous page ended by calculating the deflection of the plumb line at the 45th parallel; this page addresses why this deflection does not cause a visible discrepancy in our perception of the horizon.</meta>

->DISCOURSE ON THE CAUSE<-

would it not be necessary, that when looking toward the North, the line of the level <note>The horizontal line determined by a leveling instrument, which stays perpendicular to the plumb line.</note> should visibly sink below the Horizon? This, however, has never been observed, nor certainly does it happen. And to state the reason, which is another paradox, it is that the Earth is not entirely spherical, but rather of the shape of a sphere flattened toward the two Poles, much like an Ellipse would be if rotated upon its minor axis. This proceeds from the daily motion of the Earth, and it is a necessary consequence of the aforementioned deflection of the plumb line. Because the descent of heavy bodies is parallel to the line of this suspension <note>the plumb line</note>, the surface of any liquid must arrange itself so that this line is perpendicular to it, for otherwise, the liquid could descend further. Therefore, the surface of the sea is such that in every place the suspended thread is perpendicular to it. From which it follows that the line of the level—that is, the one which cuts the thread of the suspended lead at right angles—must mark the horizon, just as it does; only the elevation of the place where the level is set causes it to aim slightly higher. Now, the shores of landmasses being generally elevated, and almost everywhere the same in relation to the sea, it follows that the entire composite of land and sea is reduced to the same spheroidal <note>A sphere-like shape that is not perfectly round, in this case flattened at the poles like a tangerine.</note> shape that the surface of the sea necessarily assumes. And it is to be believed that the Earth took on this shape when it was first assembled by the effect of gravity, its matter possessing, from then on, a circular motion of 24 hours.

# ON GRAVITY. #

...as far as the Cape of Good Hope; and since then, having also read the very learned work of Mr. Newton, titled *The Mathematical Principles of Natural Philosophy* <note>original: *Philosophiæ Naturalis principia Mathematica*</note>; both provided me with material to further expand this Discourse. And first, regarding the different lengths of pendulums in various climates, which he also discussed, I believe I have, by means of these clocks, not only a clear confirmation of this effect of the Earth's motion, but also of the measurement of these lengths, which agrees very well with the calculation I have just provided. For having corrected and rectified, according to this calculation, the longitudes that were measured by the clocks on the return from the Cape of Good Hope to Texel in Holland (for they had not been used on the outward journey), I found that the ship's route was much better marked on the chart than it was without this correction; and so well, that upon arriving at this port, there was not an error of 5 or 6 leagues <note>A league was a unit of distance, roughly 3 miles or 4.8 kilometers.</note> in the longitude thus rectified. Assuming that the longitude of the said Cape had been accurately taken by the Jesuit Fathers <note>A Catholic religious order known for their scientific missions; they often performed astronomical observations to aid navigation.</note> when they passed through in the year 1685 on their way to Siam <note>The historical name for Thailand.</note>; and that it is 18 degrees further east than that of Paris; which I know from other sources to be very close to the truth. The details of this whole matter are set out at length in the report I made concerning this voyage of the pendulums to the aforementioned Gentlemen Directors <note>Specifically, the directors of the Dutch East India Company, who funded these navigational experiments.</note>. Upon this report, after having it examined by knowledgeable persons, they were pleased to order a second trial to be made, to ensure by several experiments the effectiveness of this invention. We shall see what the success of this other voyage will be, particularly regarding the variation of the pendulums, it being certain that, to know it well, these clocks provide a more reliable means, through their acceleration and slowing, than is that of actually measuring the length of the seconds pendulum <note>A pendulum whose period is precisely two seconds; its length changes slightly depending on the strength of gravity at different latitudes.</note> in different countries. # DISCOURSE ON THE CAUSE #

Because in the essay of which I have just spoken, the experiment has agreed so well with what I had found by reasoning, I trust it enough to want to continue this speculation, by seeking first: what then is the shape of the Earth, since, as has been said, it is not *Spherical*.

It is helpful for this purpose to consider it as entirely covered with water, or as if its entire mass were nothing else. And then it appears, from what has been explained above, that the surface must be such that, in whatever place it may be, the line that supports a plumb bob <note>A "plumb bob" is a weight, usually made of lead (plumbum), suspended from a string to determine a perfectly vertical line.</note> will meet it at right angles; taking into account gravity <note>original: *pesanteur*. In this context, it refers to the downward pull of the Earth.</note> together with the centrifugal force <note>The outward force caused by rotation.</note>, which turns the line away from its direction toward the center. This is because if the line did not meet the surface at right angles, the water could not remain in the position where it is.

### OF GRAVITY. ###

C S is to absolute gravity <note>original: *pesanteur absoluë*. This refers to gravity as it would be if the Earth were not rotating.</note> as the centrifugal force is at D; this ratio is composed of the ratio of absolute gravity to the centrifugal force at E (which is as 289 to 1) <note>Huygens is referencing a calculation, also used by Newton, showing that at the equator, the outward pull of rotation is 1/289th the strength of gravity.</note> and that of this force to the centrifugal force at D, which is as E C to D O. It appears that the nature of the curved line E D P is determined by the property of its perpendicular, such as D R; that is to say, by drawing such a perpendicular, the ratio of D C to C S must always be composed of a given ratio and that of E C to D O. Or rather, as one can easily infer, the ratio of D O to C S, or of O R to R C, must be composed of the said given ratio and that of E C to C D.

Now, it is difficult to find curved lines in this way by the given property of their perpendiculars, or, what is the same thing, by the property of their tangents. But there is a fairly easy method for this specific curve, which is based on the equilibrium of certain canals <note>Huygens is referring to imaginary tubes filled with liquid reaching from the surface to the Earth's center.</note>, an idea first provided by Mr. Newton.

The canal he proposes is represented in our figure by E C P, forming a right angle at the center of the Earth. One must conceive of it as having a narrow hollow space and being filled with water. This being the case, it is certain that the two legs, E C and C P, must remain in equilibrium if we assume that the Earth—being composed entirely of water—takes a shape where the diameters are E A and P Q. Otherwise, this water in the canal would not remain in its position even if the canal were removed, which contradicts our assumption. From this, it is easy to find the ratio of E A to P Q. For by setting E C = *a*, C P = *b*, and representing absolute gravity by a line *p*, and the centrifugal force at E by the line *n*; the weight of the canal P C is *p* times *b*, which is found by multiplying all parts <note>original: *arties*, likely an OCR error for *parties*.</note> of this canal equally by the line *p*. But the weight of the canal <note>original: *caal*, an OCR error for *canal*.</note> E C, which would be

<note>original: *Discours de la Cause de la Pesanteur*. This is Christiaan Huygens' influential treatise on the cause of gravity, published in 1690.</note>

This results in $\frac{1}{2} na$ for the total centrifugal force of the water in canal E C. When this is subtracted from its weight *pa*, there remains $pa - \frac{1}{2} na$, which must be equal to *pb*, the weight of the canal P C. From this, it appears that *a* <note>The equatorial radius.</note> is to *b* <note>The polar radius.</note> as $p$ is to $p - \frac{1}{2} n$. from *OR* to *RC* will be composed of the ratio of *p* to *n*, and of *EC* to *CD*, as can be proven through *Algebraic* calculation.

Throughout this reasoning, I have assumed that gravity is the same within the Earth as it is at its surface; this seems very plausible to me, despite the reasons one might have to doubt it, which I will speak of later. But even if it were otherwise, it would change almost nothing regarding what has been found concerning the figure of the Earth: except in the case where centrifugal force <term>centrifugal force: the outward force acting on a body moving in a circle, which Huygens believed caused the Earth to bulge</term> makes up a considerable part of gravity, or is equal to it, as in the case of the *parabolic figure*, which would then become something else entirely. Furthermore, when the centrifugal force at *E* <note>Point E represents a location on the equator.</note> is very small relative to gravity, as it is here on Earth, the *Hyperbola ETP*, because of the great distance of its center, closely approaches a *Parabola*, and consequently *EDP* <note>The curve representing the Earth's surface.</note> hardly differs from an *Ellipse*; nor does it differ much from a circle, because *EC* <note>The equatorial radius.</note> then exceeds *CP* <note>The polar radius.</note> by only a very small amount; as it was found just before, that this excess is only 1/578 of *EC*, the semi-diameter of the Earth.

Mr. Newton finds it to be 1/231 of *EC*, and thus that the shape of the Earth differs much more from a sphere; in this, he uses an entirely different calculation which I will not examine here, especially since I do not agree with a Principle that he assumes in this calculation and elsewhere; which is, that all the small parts that one can imagine in two or several different bodies, attract or tend to approach each other mutually. This I cannot admit, because I believe I see clearly that the cause of such an attraction is not explainable by any principle of *Mechanics*, nor by the rules of motion <note>Huygens, a "mechanical philosopher," believed all physical effects must be explained by direct contact or the movement of subtle matter, rejecting Newton's "action at a distance" as unscientific.</note>. Similarly, I am not persuaded of the necessity of the mutual attraction of whole bodies; having shown that, even if there were no Earth, bodies would not cease, through what is called their gravity, to tend toward a center.

<meta>catchword: tices</meta>

I have nothing, therefore, against the <term>Centripetal Force: literally "center-seeking" force; the term used by Newton to describe a force pulling objects toward a central point</term> <note>original: "Vis Centripeta"</note>, as Mr. Newton calls it, by which he makes the Planets weigh toward the Sun, and the Moon toward the Earth; instead, I agree with it without difficulty: because not only do we know by experience that there is such a manner of attraction or impulse in nature, but also that it is explained by the laws of motion, as has been seen in what I have written above concerning gravity. For nothing prevents the cause of this Centripetal Force toward the Sun from being similar to that which pushes bodies, which we call heavy, to descend toward the Earth. 

For a long time, I had imagined that the spherical shape of the Sun could be produced in the same way as that which, in my view, produces the sphericity of the Earth; but I had not extended the action of gravity to such great distances, such as from the Sun to the Planets, or from the Earth to the Moon; because the <term>Vortices: René Descartes’ theory that space is filled with a fluid matter that swirls like whirlpools, carrying the planets along</term> of Mr. Descartes, which had formerly seemed very plausible to me, and which I still had in mind, stood in the way. 

Nor had I thought of this regular decrease of gravity—namely, that it was in the <term>inverse ratio of the squares of the distances: a mathematical rule where the strength of gravity drops off significantly as distance increases (e.g., doubling the distance makes gravity four times weaker)</term> from the center: which is a new and very remarkable property of gravity, for which it is well worth seeking the reason. But seeing now, through the demonstrations of Mr. Newton, that by assuming such gravity toward the Sun, and which decreases according to the said proportion, it counterbalances the centrifugal forces of the Planets so well, and produces exactly the effect of the **Elliptical motion**, which Kepler had guessed and verified by observations, I can hardly doubt that these Hypotheses concerning gravity are true, nor that Mr. Newton’s System, insofar as it is based upon them, is likewise true. This must appear all the more probable, as one finds therein the solution to several difficulties that caused trouble in the Vor-

...tices of *Descartes*. We now see how the eccentricities <term>Eccentricity: a measurement of how much a planetary orbit deviates from a perfect circle</term> of the *Planets* can remain constantly the same; why the planes of their *Orbits* do not merge, but keep their different inclinations with respect to the plane of the *Ecliptic* <term>Ecliptic: the apparent path of the Sun across the sky; the reference plane for the solar system</term>, and why the planes of all these *Orbits* necessarily pass through the *Sun*. How the movements of the *Planets* can accelerate and slow down by the degrees observed there <note>Huygens is referring to Kepler’s Second Law, which describes how planets move faster when closer to the Sun</note>; which could hardly be such if they swam in a *Vortex* around the *Sun*. Finally, we see there how *Comets* can cross our *System*. For since it has been known that they often enter the region of the *Planets*, it was difficult to conceive how they could sometimes move in a direction contrary to that of the *Vortex*, which had enough force to carry the *Planets*. But, by the doctrine of *Mr. Newton*, this doubt is also removed; since nothing there prevents *Comets* from traveling *Elliptical* paths around the *Sun*, like the *Planets*; but paths that are more extended, and of a shape more different from a circle; and thus these bodies may have their periodic returns, as some ancient and modern *Philosophers* and *Astronomers* had imagined.

There is only this difficulty: that *Mr. Newton*, in rejecting the *Vortices* of *Descartes*, maintains that the celestial spaces contain only a very rare <note>In 17th-century science, "rare" meant thin or low-density, the opposite of "dense"</note> matter, so that the *Planets* and *Comets* encounter as little obstacle as possible in their course. This rarity being assumed, it does not seem possible to explain either the action of *Gravity*, or that of *Light*, at least by the means I have used <note>Huygens is famous for his "Wave Theory of Light," which required a medium (the aether) to carry the waves; he is worried that if space is empty, his theory of light won't work</note>. To examine this point, I say that the ethereal matter can be considered rare in two ways: namely, either that its particles are distant from each other, with much void between them; or that they touch each other, but the texture <note>original: "tissu"</note> of each is rare, and

interspersed with many small empty spaces. As for the vacuum <term>Vacuum: a space entirely devoid of matter</term>, I admit it without difficulty, and I even believe it necessary for the movement of small corpuscles <term>Corpuscles: minute particles of matter</term> among themselves. I am not of the opinion of Mr. *Descartes*, who maintains that extension <term>Extension: the property of taking up space; Descartes argued that if there is space, there must be matter</term> alone constitutes the essence of the body; rather, I add to it a perfect hardness, which renders it impenetrable and incapable of being broken or chipped. However, considering the rarity of the first manner <note>Huygens is referring to a model where particles are scattered far apart in a vacuum</note>, I do not see how one could then give an account of Gravity; and as for Light, it seems to me entirely impossible, with such vacuums, to explain its prodigious speed, which must be six hundred thousand times greater than that of Sound, according to the demonstration by Mr. *Rømer* <note>Ole Rømer (1644–1710), the Danish astronomer who first proved that light travels at a finite speed by observing the moons of Jupiter</note>, which I reported in the *Treatise on Light*. This is why I hold that such rarity could not be suited to the celestial spaces.

There is more likelihood in conceiving it the other way; because the particles can touch there, as I supposed in the aforementioned *Treatise*, and yet, because of the lightness of their weave <note>Huygens imagines a "mesh" or "lattice" of particles that are hard but have lots of "holes" in their structure, like a net</note>, they offer very little resistance to the movement of the Planets. For who knows how far nature can go in composing hard bodies with little matter; especially if very minute and slender particles, or even hollow ones, can be infinitely strong. But I believe that, without considering rarity, the great agitation of the ethereal matter <term>Ethereal matter: the "ether," a subtle substance then believed to fill all space and transmit light waves</term> can contribute much to its penetrability. For if the small movement of the particles of water makes it liquid, and of much less resistance regarding the bodies that swim in it than is sand or some very fine powder; must it not be that a more subtle matter, and infinitely more agitated, is also that much easier to penetrate?

Whatever the case may be, we see that nature does not lack industry in ensuring that there are spaces in which bodies move with very little resistance; for that appears

[This] would be evident from what our hands feel in the air, and even more so by the experiments conducted in glass vessels from which all the air has been removed <note>Huygens refers to the air pump, a device used to create a vacuum to study physics</note>; where the lightest feather descends with the same speed as a lead ball. If one wished to maintain that this proceeds from the great rarity of the matter remaining in this vacuum of air, I would allege on the contrary that one perceives there the effect of a matter that weighs quite considerably, as was seen in the experiment reported above.

As for the reasoning of **Mr. Newton** in Proposition 6 of Book 3 <note>Referring to Newton's *Philosophiæ Naturalis Principia Mathematica* (1687)</note> to prove the extreme rarity of the ether <term>Ether: a theoretical substance once thought to fill all of space</term>: namely, that the weights of bodies are proportional to the quantities of matter they contain; and that, this being the case, if the spaces of air or ether were as full of matter as gold and silver, these metals would not descend through them; because a solid body, not having a greater <term>Specific gravity: the density of an object compared to the fluid it is in</term> specific gravity than a fluid, would not be able to sink into it. I say that I agree that the weights of bodies follow the quantities of their matter; and I have even demonstrated this in this present *Discourse*. But I have also shown that for these bodies we call "heavy," gravity can indeed be imprinted by the centrifugal force <term>Centrifugal force: the outward force felt by an object moving in a circle; Huygens theorized that a swirling fluid pushes objects toward the center</term> of a matter which does not itself weigh toward the center of the Earth because of its very rapid circular motion, but which tends to move away from it. This matter, therefore, can very well fill all the space around the Earth that other corpuscles <term>Corpuscles: tiny particles of matter</term> do not occupy, without this preventing the descent of bodies called heavy; being, on the contrary, the only cause that compels them to do so. It would be a different matter if one supposed that gravity was an inherent quality of corporeal matter. But that is something I do not believe **Mr. Newton** would agree to, because such a hypothesis would lead us far away from Mathematical or Mechanical principles <note>Huygens is arguing that gravity must have a physical, mechanical cause (like moving particles) rather than being a "magical" property of matter itself</note>.

He will perhaps tell me that, even if I were granted...

...directly from the window; one sees how easy it is to be deceived by it, because of the multitude of repeated reflections which occur as if in an instant; so that the Sound, which is heard as coming immediately from the open window, may actually come from it, or from places very nearby, after a double reflection. I admit, then, that as for the ripples or circles that form on the surface of the water, the matter occurs much as Mr. Newton asserts: that is to say, that a wave, after having passed through an opening, then expands to one side and the other, and yet more weakly there than in the middle. After which I will add what I have found among my papers concerning the movement of bodies through the air, or another resisting medium; a movement which he treats at length in Book 2.

That is to say, these pendulums would be as 288 to 289; or that the pendulum at the Equator would be shorter by $\frac{1}{289}$ than it would be at the Pole. This is exactly the same difference that previously resulted from the daily rotation, or centrifugal force. So that a Clock, with the same length of pendulum, would go more slowly at the Equator than at the Pole by double the amount it was slowed by the movement of the Earth; and thus this daily difference at the Equator would be nearly 5 minutes. And under other parallels, it would everywhere be found to be more than double what it was before. On the contrary, according to Mr. Newton, gravity inside the Earth decreases as bodies approach the center; but he uses his own principle to prove this, with which I have said I do not agree. <note>Newton argued that gravity is a universal attraction between all particles of matter; Huygens rejected this "action at a distance," preferring to explain gravity through the pressure of a spinning celestial fluid.</note>

Things which at first seem very far from our knowledge; and which yet are consequences of the principles I reported shortly before.

This determination takes place in the Planets that have one or more Satellites, because their periodic times, and their distances from the Planets they accompany, must enter into the calculation. ...whereas, according to the dimensions provided by Mr. Cassini <note>Giovanni Domenico Cassini, the director of the Paris Observatory, whose measurements of planetary distances were more accurate than previous estimates.</note>, it is approximately 10,000, which is close enough to what I had previously found, through plausible reasons, in my *System of Saturn* <note>original: *Sÿsteme de Saturne* — referring to Huygens' 1659 work *Systema Saturnium*.</note>, namely 12,000. I also differ somewhat regarding the diameters of the Planets. Thus, by my calculation, the **gravity** <term>gravity</term><note>original: *pesanteur*. Huygens uses this to describe the mechanical force pushing objects downward.</note> on **Jupiter**, compared to that which we have here on **Earth**, is found to be as 13 to 10, whereas Mr. Newton makes them equal, or only imperceptibly different. But the gravity on the **Sun**, which, according to the numbers just seen, was about 12 times greater than our own on Earth, I find to be 26 times greater. From this it follows, by explaining gravity in the manner I have done, that the **fluid matter** <note>In Huygens' theory, gravity is caused by a "fluid matter" or ether that revolves rapidly around celestial bodies, pushing ordinary matter toward the center through centrifugal effects.</note> near the Sun must have a speed 49 times greater than that which we found near the Earth; which was already 17 times greater than the speed of a point on the Equator. This is, then, a terrifying rapidity; which made me consider whether it might well be the cause of the **Sun's brilliant light**, assuming that light is produced as I explain in my writings on the subject; namely, that the Solar particles, swimming in a more subtle and extremely agitated matter, strike against the particles of the **Ether** surrounding them. For if the agitation of such matter, with the movement it has here on Earth, can cause the brightness of a **candle flame**, or of burning **Camphor**, how much greater will that brightness be from a movement 49 times swifter and more violent?

I have seen with pleasure what Mr. Newton writes regarding the **falls and the trajectories of heavy bodies** in the air, or in some other medium that resists movement; having myself applied the same research in the past. And since this subject belongs in part to that of Gravity, I believe I can report here what I discovered then. This, however, I shall do only in **abridged** fashion.

# ON GRAVITY

abridged and without adding the demonstrations; having neglected to complete them, because this speculation did not seem to me useful enough, nor of consequence, in proportion to the difficulty encountered therein.

I first examined these movements by assuming that the forces of **Resistance** <term>Resistance</term><note>Fluid resistance or drag, which opposes the motion of an object as it moves through a medium like air or water.</note> are proportional to the **Velocities** of the bodies, which at that time seemed very likely to me. But having obtained what I was seeking, I learned almost at the same time, through the experiments we conducted in Paris at the **Academy of Sciences**, that the resistance of air and water was proportional to the squares of the velocities. And the reason is easy enough to conceive; because a body, traveling for example with double velocity, is met by twice as many particles of air or water, and with double speed. Thus I saw my new Theory overturned, or at least useless. After which I also wished to seek what happens when one assumes this true foundation of Resistances; where I saw that the matter was much more difficult, especially regarding the curved line traversed by bodies thrown obliquely.

In the first supposition, where the resistances are proportional to the velocities, I noticed that, to find the spaces passed through in certain times when bodies fall or rise perpendicularly, and to know the velocities at the end of those times, there was a curved line, which I had examined long ago, that was of great use in this research. It may be called the *Logarithmic* or the *Logistic* <note>Today we know this simply as the logarithmic curve. Huygens was one of the early mathematicians to explore its physical applications.</note>, for I do not see that any name has yet been given to it, although others have considered it before. This infinite line being *ABC*, it has a straight line for an **Asymptote** <term>Asymptote</term><note>A straight line that a curve approaches indefinitely but never touches.</note>, such as *DE*; in which if one takes any equal consecutive parts, such as *DG*, *GF*, and draws perpendiculars from the points *D*, *G*, *F* to the curve, name-

To explain what concerns the **falling of bodies** <term>falling of bodies</term><note>The study of kinematics regarding how objects accelerate under the influence of gravity while resisting air.</note>, I repeat here first what I wrote at the end of the *Treatise on the Center of Oscillation* <note>Original: *Traité du Centre d'Agitation*. This refers to Huygens' landmark 1673 work *Horologium Oscillatorium*, where he explored the physics of pendulums and moving bodies.</note>: namely that a body, in falling through the air, continually increases its speed, but however in such a way that it can never exceed, nor even reach, a certain degree; which is the speed that would be required for the air to blow from bottom to top, to keep the body suspended without being able to descend; for then, the force of the air against this body equals its **weight** <term>weight</term><note>Huygens uses *pesanteur*, which refers to the downward force of gravity acting on the mass of the object.</note>. I call this speed, for each body, the **Terminal** velocity.

If therefore a heavy body is thrown perpendicularly upward, with a velocity whose ratio to the **Terminal** velocity is given, for example as from the part AK to KD in the **ordinate** <term>ordinate</term><note>The vertical coordinate or a line segment representing a value in a geometric construction.</note> AD, perpendicular to the **asymptote** <term>asymptote</term><note>A line that a curve approaches closer and closer but never actually touches.</note> DE; let XB be drawn parallel to this asymptote, & let the curve be touched at point B by the straight line BO, which meets DE at O, & DA at Q. Huygens is referencing Newton's *Principia Mathematica*, published in 1687, which revolutionized physics.</note> Treatise that I took it up again, to see if what we had sought through very different paths agreed as they should. That the infinite space between an **ordinate** <term>ordinate</term><note>The vertical distance from a point on the curve to the horizontal axis (the asymptote).</note>, the **Logistics curve**, and its **asymptote** <term>asymptote</term><note>A line that a curve approaches closer and closer but never touches.</note>, on the side where these last two approach each other, is double the triangle formed by the ordinate, the tangent drawn from the same point as the ordinate, and the **subtangent** <term>subtangent</term><note>The segment on the asymptote defined by the intersection of the tangent line and the ordinate.
