8. That the space comprised between two ordinates is equal to the rectangle of the subtangent and the difference between those same ordinates. Thus, in the same figure, the space *A D F B* is equal to the rectangle formed by the subtangent *F O* and the segment *K A*.

9. That the **solid** <note>In this context, a "solid" refers to a three-dimensional volume created by rotating a two-dimensional shape around an axis.</note> created by the infinite space starting from an ordinate, when revolving around the asymptote, is **sesquialter** <note>original: "sesquialtere"; a ratio of 3 to 2, or 1.5 times the volume.</note> to the Cone whose height is equal to the subtangent and whose base radius is equal to the same ordinate. Thus, the solid formed by the infinite space *B F O C*, revolving around *F O*, is sesquialter to the cone formed by triangle *B F O* revolving around that same line *F O*.

10. That the solid produced by the same infinite space, when revolving around the ordinate *B F* from which it begins, is six times the cone formed by the triangle *B F O* by its <unclear>rotation</unclear> upon *B F*. From which measurement of solids it follows:

11. That the **center of gravity** <note>The point at which the weight of the geometric space or solid is perfectly balanced.</note> of the infinite space, measured from an ordinate, is distant from that ordinate by the length of the subtangent.

12. That this same center of gravity is distant from the asymptote by one-quarter of the ordinate.

13. I had also found that the center of gravity of the first of the aforementioned infinite solids is distant from its base by half the length of the subtangent.

14. And that the center of gravity of the other solid is distant from its infinite base by one-eighth of its axis.

15. It is well enough known that this Logistics line serves for the **Quadrature** <term>quadrature</term><note>The historical term for calculating the area of a shape, specifically the process of determining a square with an area equal to that of a curved figure.</note>—

<gloss>logarithmic curve, ordinate, asymptote, subtangent, three-dimensional volume, center of gravity, area calculation</gloss>

<summary>Huygens outlines several geometric proofs regarding the logarithmic curve, defining the proportions of infinite areas, volumes of revolution, and the locations of their centers of gravity. These mathematical properties serve as the foundation for his mechanical explanation of how gravity operates.</summary>
<keywords>logarithmic curve, Logistics line, subtangent, asymptote, solids of revolution, center of gravity, quadrature, geometry, Christiaan Huygens</keywords>

--- Page 195 ---
<meta>This page continues the geometric and mathematical proofs regarding the properties of curves, specifically linking the "Logistics line" discussed on previous pages to the quadrature of the hyperbola. This is part of Huygens' broader argument about the nature of gravity and centrifugal force.</meta>

# ON GRAVITY #

[Qua]drature <note>The "quadrature" of a shape is the mathematical process of determining its area. The word begins mid-sentence, continuing from the previous page's catchword.</note> of the **Hyperbola** <term>Hyperbola</term><note>A type of smooth curve lying in a plane, defined by its geometric properties; here, Huygens is interested in the area beneath it.</note>, following the demonstrations of Father Greg. de St. Vincent <note>Grégoire de Saint-Vincent (1584–1667) was a Jesuit mathematician who discovered that the area under a hyperbola behaves logarithmically.</note> regarding the **hyperbolic spaces** comprised between two **ordinates** <term>ordinates</term><note>Vertical lines extending from the horizontal axis to the curve.</note> on one of the **asymptotes** <term>asymptotes</term><note>A straight line that a curve approaches closer and closer but never actually touches.</note>. And that if there are two such spaces, where the ordinates of one are in the same ratio as *AD* to *HG* in the last figure, and the ordinates of the other as *BF* to *CE*; these spaces will be to one another as the lines *DG* to *FE*.

<image-desc>A geometric diagram illustrating properties of a hyperbola. The curve passes through points A, H, B, and C. A vertical line on the left represents an asymptote with points D, G, F, O, and E marked. Horizontal segments extend from these points to the curve and to other vertical lines. Various intersection points are labeled with letters including K, X, Q, R, N, P, L, and M, forming a series of rectangles and segments related to the hyperbolic area. A small mark or asterisk is visible in the space between points Q, A, and H.</image-desc>

But it has not been noticed, as far as I know, that these same hyperbolic spaces are to the **Parallelogram of the Hyperbola** (I call it such, the parallelogram whose sides are the two ordinates on the asymptotes, drawn from a single point of the Section) as each of the lines *DG*, *FE*, is to the **subtangent** <term>subtangent</term><note>A segment on the axis defined by the intersection of the tangent line to the curve and the ordinate.</note> *FO*. So that, if the Parallelogram of the Hyperbola is assumed to be 0.4342944819 parts <note>This specific number is the modulus of common (Briggsian) logarithms, or log10(e). Huygens is linking the geometry of the hyperbola directly to the calculation of logarithms.</note>, each hyperbolic space, comprised between two ordinates to one of the asymptotes, will be to this parallelogram as the **Logarithm** of the ratio of the same ordinates—that is to say, as the difference of the Logarithms of the numbers expressing the ratio of the ordinates—is to the number 0.4342944819; taking Logarithms of 10 characters besides the **characteristic** <term>characteristic</term><note>In early logarithm tables, the "characteristic" refers to the integer part of the logarithm, while the "mantissa" refers to the decimal part.</note>.

<meta>catchword: And</meta>

<summary>Huygens explains the relationship between the area under a hyperbola and logarithmic values, building on the work of Grégoire de Saint-Vincent. He provides a specific constant (the modulus) to relate hyperbolic spaces to common logarithms.</summary>
<keywords>hyperbola, quadrature, Grégoire de Saint-Vincent, logarithms, asymptotes, subtangent, geometry, mathematical modulus</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the "Discourse on the Cause of Gravity" by Christiaan Huygens. It connects the mathematical findings of this work back to his previous major publication on mechanics, "Horologium Oscillatorium" (1673), specifically referencing his work on the geometry of curves. This provides a formal conclusion before listing the errata for both the "Treatise on Light" and the "Discourse on Gravity."</meta>

# DISCOURSE ON THE CAUSE [OF GRAVITY] ETC. #

And from this it is easy to verify the Quadrature <term>Quadrature</term><note>The mathematical process of determining the area of a geometric figure, effectively a precursor to modern integration.</note> of the **Hyperbola** <term>Hyperbola</term><note>A specific type of smooth, open curve; Huygens is referring to the calculation of the area beneath this curve.</note> that I gave in the *Treatise on the Evolution of Curved Lines*, which is in my *The Pendulum Clock* <note>original: "Horologium Oscillatorium," Huygens’ landmark 1673 book on the physics of pendulums and the geometry of curves.</note>.

->T H E   E N D.<-

### *Errors to be corrected in the Treatise on Light.*

**Page 9, line 1.** read: being. & **line 17.** read: this. **Page 19, line 4.** read: nevertheless. **Page 20, line 13.** read: properties. **Page 43, line 13.** read: the air. **Page 59, line 15.** read: comprised. **Page 60, line 16.** read: I placed. **Page 63, line 25.** read: is very close. **Page 76, lines 1, 28, 29.** for rectangle, read: parallelogram. **Page 80, last line**, read: proportional. **Page 81, line 4.** for No. 53. read: No. 34 <note>blotted text</note> **Page 97, line 21.** read: hypotenuse. **Page 98, line 1.** instead of "is CH", read: "let it be CH". **Page 116, line 2.** instead of "divides" read: "directs". **Page 117, line 6.** read: will come together. **Page 121, line 13.** for Dv read: Fv.
